# On the Renormalization Group Flow of Active Flocks

**arXiv ID**: 2606.20552v2
**Authors**: Kevin T. Grosvenor, Subodh P. Patil
**Published**: 2026-06-18
**Categories**: cond-mat.soft, cond-mat.stat-mech, hep-th
**Comments**: A mistake in the previous version regarding symmetry structure of the theory corrected and elaborated upon, conclusions unchanged. References added and discussion amended. 19 pages, six appendices
**HTML URL**: https://arxiv.org/html/2606.20552v2

## Abstract

In this paper, we study the statistical field-theoretic renormalization of active flocks via the MSRDJ action formulation for stochastic systems, focusing on the Toner-Tu theory of `Malthusian flocks', or polar-ordered, momentum non-conserving active fluids where relaxation times for density fluctuations are so short that they can be eliminated as a hydrodynamic variable. Working in the limit of isotropic diffusion in two spatial dimensions, we compute the renormalization of the couplings and their anomalous dimensions to all orders, facilitated by the Ward identities associated with the non-linear realization of a diagonal rotation/ shift symmetry, and a non-local field-dependent redundancy specific to the MSRDJ representation of this model. We find a range of behavior depending on the parameters of the theory. If $κ$ is the diffusion coefficient and $Δ$ is the variance of the noise, we find a line of fixed points and a marginal vertex instability at $Δ/κ= 2π$. This instability separates Gaussian, and symmetry-protected strongly interacting gapless phases, realizing non-equilibrium critical behavior beyond standard Wilson--Fisher criticality, and implies the persistence of long range order when $Δ/κ$ is below the critical value. We revisit and contextualize various claims and counter-claims in the literature in light of our findings, and discuss extensions of our analysis to flocks with anisotropic diffusion, and where density fluctuations are reintroduced.

## On the Renormalization Group Flow of Active FlocksKevin T. Grosvenor1ktgrosvenor@up.edu.phSubodh P. Patil2patil@lorentz.leidenuniv.nl1National Institute of Physics, University of the Philippines, Diliman, Quezon City 1101, Philippines2Instituut-Lorentz for Theoretical Physics, Leiden University, 2333 CA, Leiden, The Netherlands

## Abstract

In this paper, we study the statistical field-theoretic renormalization of active flocks via the MSRDJ action formulation for stochastic systems, focusing on the Toner-Tu theory of ‘Malthusian flocks’, or polar-ordered, momentum non-conserving active fluids where relaxation times for density fluctuations are so short that they can be eliminated as a hydrodynamic variable. Working in the limit of isotropic diffusion in two spatial dimensions, we compute the renormalization of the couplings and their anomalous dimensions to all orders, facilitated by the Ward identities associated with the non-linear realization of a diagonal rotation/ shift symmetry, and a non-local field-dependent redundancy specific to the MSRDJ representation of this model. We find a range of behavior depending on the parameters of the theory. Ifκ\kappais the diffusion coefficient andΔ\Deltais the variance of the noise, we find a line of fixed points and a marginal vertex instability atΔ/κ=2​π\Delta/\kappa=2\pi. This instability separates Gaussian, and symmetry-protected strongly interacting gapless phases, realizing non-equilibrium critical behavior beyond standard Wilson–Fisher criticality, and implies the persistence of long range order whenΔ/κ\Delta/\kappais below the critical value. We revisit and contextualize various claims and counter-claims in the literature in light of our findings, and discuss extensions of our analysis to flocks with anisotropic diffusion, and where density fluctuations are reintroduced.

## IPreliminary remarks

Recently, there has been renewed interest and debate over how hydrodynamic descriptions of two-dimensional polar flocks behave under renormalization, and in particular, the conditions under which long range order can persist in such systems[9,25,13,10,12,11]. These are all framed within the context of the Toner-Tu hydrodynamics of active flocks, within which it was first established that motile polar systems can evade the Coleman-Hohenberg-Mermin-Wagner constraints that forbid long-range order in equilibrium two-dimensional continuous-spin systems[46,47,45,48,34,49]. That framework naturally lends itself to the language of critical exponents and canonical scaling predictions. The long-wavelength, long-time scaling of two-dimensional polar flocks is customarily characterized by three critical exponents: the dynamical critical exponentzz(so that time scales ast∼yzt\sim y^{z}, whereyyis a distance transverse to the flock propagation), the anisotropy exponentζ\zeta(x∼yζx\sim y^{\zeta}, wherexxis a longitudinal distance), and the roughness exponentχ\chi(δ​θ∼yχ\delta\theta\sim y^{\chi}, whereδ​θ\delta\thetaare fluctuations in the flocking direction).

In the case where one takes theMalthusianlimit – where density fluctuations are on such short timescales that they can be eliminated as a hydrodynamic variable – Chaté and Solon[9,10,11]argue that a generalized Galilean invariance protects the nonlinear couplings of the theory, denotedλx\lambda_{x}andλy\lambda_{y}from receiving corrections111We define the Malthusian flock and its parameters in the next section. For the present discussion,λx\lambda_{x}andλy\lambda_{y}can be taken to parameterize the derivatively coupled operatorsλx​∂xψ\lambda_{x}\partial_{x}\psiandλy​∂yψ\lambda_{y}\partial_{y}\psi, whereψ\psiis the flock angle, and where each operator can be dressed by powers or functions ofψ\psi., leading to three relations among the three critical exponents, and therefore exact exponent values. On the other hand, although Chen et al.[13,12]agree with two of the resulting exponent relations, they contest the claim that symmetries forceλx\lambda_{x}andλy\lambda_{y}to have vanishing anomalous dimensions. Consequently, they obtain only two independent exponent relations and conclude that the long wavelength properties of the Malthusian-flock cannot be determined exactly from symmetry arguments alone. Both groups, however, agree that the variance of the noise that the flock is subject to, denotedΔ\Delta, receives no explicit diagrammatic corrections, and therefore does not acquire an anomalous dimension.

In the context of immortal (i.e. number density conserving) flocks, the divergence is more substantial. Chen et al. argue that the known symmetries of the flocking equations are insufficient to derive any exact relations among the critical exponents, implying that no exact exponent values can presently be obtained. Chaté and Solon on the other hand, while not explicitly deriving exact exponent relations for immortal flocks, contend that Chen et al.’s renormalization-group analysis does not establish their no-go conclusion and therefore do not rule out the possibility that additional symmetry-based constraints remain to be discovered. In summary, while there is broad agreement that the Malthusian limit admits more constraints, the number-conserving case is evidently rather more unsettled, and there does not yet appear to be consensus on universal limitations as to how large, or how long-lived active flocks can become. More recent numerical and quantitative assessments of the predictions for hydrodynamic limits of flocks (continuum simulations and microscopic Vicsek-type models) have been crucial in testing analytic claims[18,41,42,33,43,36]. These numerical studies are central to the current debate because finite-size effects, implementation differences between Malthusian and number-conserving systems, and the treatment of non-linearities all affect the interpretation of measured exponents.

At its core, the debate hinges upon consistent and complete renormalization group (RG) analyses of the theory. However, one is confronted with a number of difficulties in interpreting prior analyses as they have nominally been discussed in the literature. Firstly, while guided by essentially correct physical intuition, conclusions drawn solely from dynamical scaling arguments, or from phenomenological renormalization schemes such as dynamical RG should be interpreted with caution, and mindful of a number of caveats. Moreover, symmetry arguments by themselves do not justify the all-orders non-renormalization statements needed in order to derive the exact exponent relations, and ought to be established explicitly via Ward identities rather than presumed. In contrast, a complete functional or diagrammatic renormalization analysis would provide a controlled framework in which all symmetry-allowed operators generated under coarse-graining can be systematically identified and their contributions explicitly evaluated.

Secondly, previous analyses have made the seemingly a priori reasonable assumption that the specific form of the equations of motion (involving derivatively coupled trigonometric functions of the flock angleψ\psi) will deform under renormalization, going so far as claiming that one is guaranteed to flow away from these special functional forms. If that is the case, then the RG analysis must be taken from the more general starting point with all possible higher-point interactions included in a consistent power counting expansion (as one does in any effective field theoretic treatment), rather than the specific ones that derive from the original Toner-Tu equation of motion. However, from this more general standpoint, the interpretation of the order parameter as a director angle for the flock becomes tenuous without additional qualifications. One additionally encounters analyses that invoke the assumption that any dimensionally regularized computation in4−ϵ4-\epsilondimensions can be extrapolated down to 2 dimensions. These extrapolations are used to infer that corrections to the diffusion constants are all positive. While this may seem plausible given the validity of the extrapolation, this is far from proven, and as will be elaborated upon further, turns out to not be the case in general.

Clearly, it behooves us to perform a consistent renormalization of any given hydrodynamical description of active flocks to completion before one can attempt to meaningfully address any of the questions being debated. In what follows, we aim to do precisely this by adopting the so-called Martin-Siggia-Rose-De Dominicis-Janssen (MSRDJ) action formulation for stochastic systems[35,27,15], and performing an order by order renormalization of all propagators and interaction vertices in order to determine the RG running of a particular limit of the theory of Malthusian flocks.

We should be clear about what our investigation will not attempt: The treatment that follows does not compute the RG flow of the full theory of Malthusian flocks, nor any extension to the theory of immortal flocks. We restrict our attention in what follows to the theory of Malthusian flocks in the limit of isotropic diffusion. That is, ifκ||\kappa_{||}andκ⟂\kappa_{\perp}are the diffusion constants parallel and perpendicular to the direction of net motion of the flock, we only consider the limitκ||=κ⟂\kappa_{||}=\kappa_{\perp}.

Not only will the isotropic limit turn out to be preserved under renormalization, it represents a limit of the theory that facilitates a renormalization of the couplings and anomalous dimensions of the theory to all loop order. The workhorse for the remarkable prooperties of the isotropic Malthusian flock will turn out to be the non-linearly realized nature of a diagonal flock angle shift/ compensating spatial rotation symmetry, along with a non-local field-dependent redundancy of the MSRDJ formulation specific to the model222A previous version of this preprint mistakenly identified this as a generalizedGalileonsymmetry[39,38], which was inferred from incorrectly implemented symmetry transformations. Rectifying the error resulted in an even larger set of invariances that characterize the isotropic Malthusian flock, which supply the necessary ingredients to relate the anomalous dimensions of the couplings of the theory, as discussed in SectionIVand AppendixD. Although our results and conclusions are unchanged, the full physical implications of the field redundancy remains an open question to us., the details of which will be expanded upon in SectionIV333It turns out that the diagonal shift/rotation subgroup discussed in SectionIVwill still be preserved even in the anisotropic case (i.e. whenκ||≠κ⟂\kappa_{||}\neq\kappa_{\perp}), but at the cost of introducing two additional non-linear interaction terms, each with its own coupling constant. We thank Sriram Ramaswamy and Ananyo Maitra for sharing this fact with us. We discuss extensions of our analysis to the anisotropic case in our concluding remarks.. The main technical results that will follow in the subsequent sections are summarized as:
- •

By recasting the stochastic differential equations that define the isotropic limit of the Malthusian flock into the MSRDJ formalism, we arrive at a statistical field theoretic setup in which we can perform an order-by-order perturbative renormalization of the theory.
- •

We explicitly find numerous diagrammatic cancellations and non-renormalizations, which trace their origins to the underlying kinematics, redundancies, and symmetries of the theory, which are, moreover, non-linearly realized.
- •

As a result, the functional forms of the derivatively coupled sine and cosine advection potentials are preserved to all orders under renormalization, and are protected by a Ward identity associated with a diagonal subgroup where flock angle shifts are locked to spatial rotations.
- •

We find thatλ\lambdaacquires an anomalous dimension which depends on the noise and diffusion coefficients (Δ\Deltaandκ\kappa, respectively) such that a range of behavior is possible, where a symmetry-protected strongly coupled gapless phase is separated from a gapless Gaussian phase by a line of critical points atΔ/κ=2​π\Delta/\kappa=2\pi.
- •

Additional Ward identities associated with redundancies of the MSRDJ formulation of the isotropic Malthusian flock forceΔ\Deltato acquire the same anomalous dimension asλ\lambdaandκ\kappa, even asΔ\Deltaandκ\kappareceive no explicit diagrammatic corrections.

The latter point hinges on a subtlety regarding the renormalization procedure in boost-non-invariant settings that we expand upon in AppendixD. The properties of the stochastic fluctuations, in particular, its variance also gets renormalized, as we argue must be the case in an interacting theory. Our main conclusion, as discussed in SectionVis that quasi-long-range order is indeed possible when the RG invariant ratioΔ/κ\Delta/\kappais below the critical valueΔ/κ=2​π\Delta/\kappa=2\pi, and that many of the special properties of the theory and its phases can indeed be understood to follow from its kinematics, symmetries and field redundancies alone. We conclude our investigation with a discussion of the wider significance of our findings away from this very symmetric, and evidently rather special limit of the Malthusian flock444It became incumbent upon us over the course of our study to identify where and how the treatment and conclusions in[9,25,13,10,12,11]diverged from each other, and to that of our own analysis. In the interest of clarity, we address certain key points in our concluding remarks and appendices, and welcome correspondence for additional clarification..

## IISetup

In this section, we elaborate on the set up within which we renormalize the theory of Malthusian flocks, utilizing the MSRDJ formalism as our workhorse[35,27,15]. We begin with the hydrodynamic equation for the local direction angleψ\psiof a two dimensional flock, in terms of which we define the unit vectors longitudinal and transverse to the flocking direction:𝐧^\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{n}}=(cos⁡ψsin⁡ψ),\displaystyle=\binom{\cos\psi}{\sin\psi},𝐧^⟂\displaystyle\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}=(−sin⁡ψcos⁡ψ).\displaystyle=\binom{-\sin\psi}{\cos\psi}.(1)

In order to proceed, we perform a background field expansion and take the net motion of the flock to be in the positivexx-direction (cf. the discussion surrounding (92) in AppendixA). This will be without loss of generality given the symmetries of the system, to be detailed in SectionIV. Therefore,ψ\psidenotes the local perturbation of the fluid flow away from net motion along thexx-axis, so thatkxk_{x}andkyk_{y}may always be thought of ask||k_{||}andk⟂k_{\perp}, the momenta parallel and perpendicular to the flocking direction.

The equation of motion is given in eq. III.6 of[13]:∂tψ\displaystyle\partial_{t}\psi=λ​∇⋅𝐧^⟂+κ​∇2ψ+ξ,\displaystyle=\lambda\nabla\cdot\hat{{\mathbf{n}}}_{\perp}+\kappa\nabla^{2}\psi+\xi,(2)

whereλ\lambdaandκ\kappaare constants andξ\xiis taken to be Gaussian noise, with zero mean and two-point function⟨ξ​(𝐱,t)​ξ​(𝐱′,t′)⟩=2​Δ​δ​(𝐱−𝐱′)​δ​(t−t′),\bigl\langle\xi({\mathbf{x}},t)\,\xi({\mathbf{x}}^{\prime},t^{\prime})\bigr\rangle=2\Delta\delta({\mathbf{x}}-{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\delta(t-t^{\prime}),(3)

whereΔ\Deltais a constant (half the variance of the noise). The coefficientκ\kappais the diffusion constant and can, more generally, be different along the two spatial directions. Here, we stick to the spatially isotropic case as a matter of conceptual clarity and simplicity. We discuss how to extend our calculations to the anisotropic case in our concluding remarks, along with extensions where density fluctuations are re-admitted as a hydrodynamic variable.

With the definitionℰ≡∂tψ−λ​∇⋅𝐧^⟂−κ​∇2ψ,{\cal E}\equiv\partial_{t}\psi-\lambda\nabla\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}-\kappa\nabla^{2}\psi,(4)

the equation of motion (2) now readsℰ−ξ=0{\cal E}-\xi=0. The partition function for this system is the path integral over all configurations ofψ\psiand the noise constrained by the equation of motion:Z=∫𝒟​ψ​𝒟​ξ​e−14​Δ​∫𝑑t​dd​x​ξ2​δ​(ℰ−ξ).Z=\int{\cal D}\psi\,{\cal D}\xi\,e^{-\frac{1}{4\Delta}\int dt\,d^{d}x\,\xi^{2}}\delta({\cal E}-\xi).(5)

The integral over the noise is trivial, of course, but the result will be more tractable if we integrate in an auxiliary field by Fourier transforming the delta function:Z=∫𝒟​ψ​𝒟​χ​𝒟​ξ​exp⁡[−∫𝑑t​dd​x​(14​Δ​ξ2−i​χ​(ℰ−ξ))]Z=\int{\cal D}\psi\,{\cal D}\chi\,{\cal D}\xi\,\exp\biggl[-\int dt\,d^{d}x\biggl(\frac{1}{4\Delta}\xi^{2}-i\chi({\cal E}-\xi)\biggr)\biggr](6)

Now,ξ\ximay be integrated out leavingZ=∫𝒟​ψ​𝒟​χ​e−∫𝑑t​dd​x​(Δ​χ2−i​χ​ℰ)=∫𝒟​ψ​𝒟​χ​e−S,Z=\int{\cal D}\psi\,{\cal D}\chi\,e^{-\int dt\,d^{d}x(\Delta\chi^{2}-i\chi{\cal E})}=\int{\cal D}\psi\,{\cal D}\chi\,e^{-S},(7)

where the actionSSis the spacetime integral of the Lagrangian density,ℒ=Δ​χ2−i​χ​ℰ.{\cal L}=\Delta\chi^{2}-i\chi{\cal E}.(8)

The Euler-Lagrange equations of motion read𝒜\displaystyle\mathscr{A}≡ℰ+2​i​Δ​χ=0,\displaystyle\equiv{\cal E}+2i\Delta\chi=0,ℬ\displaystyle\mathscr{B}=∂tχ+λ​∂||χ+κ​∇2χ=0,\displaystyle=\partial_{t}\chi+\lambda\partial_{||}\chi+\kappa\nabla^{2}\chi=0,(9)

where the longitudinal derivative is∂||χ=𝐧^⋅∇χ=(cos⁡ψ)​∂xχ+(sin⁡ψ)​∂yχ.\partial_{||}\chi=\hat{\mathbf{n}}\cdot\nabla\chi=(\cos\psi)\partial_{x}\chi+(\sin\psi)\partial_{y}\chi.(10)

The equation of motion𝒜=0\mathscr{A}=0implies thatχ\chimust be pure imaginary ifψ\psiis to be real. Therefore, we define the real fieldϕ\phito beϕ=−i​χ,\phi=-i\chi,(11)

so thatℒ=ϕ​ℰ−Δ​ϕ2.{\cal L}=\phi{\cal E}-\Delta\phi^{2}.(12)

This way, the equations of motion become𝒜\displaystyle\mathscr{A}≡ℰ−2​Δ​ϕ=0,\displaystyle\equiv{\cal E}-2\Delta\phi=0,ℬ\displaystyle\mathscr{B}≡∂tϕ+λ​∂||ϕ+κ​∇2ϕ=0.\displaystyle\equiv\partial_{t}\phi+\lambda\partial_{||}\phi+\kappa\nabla^{2}\phi=0.(13)

Changing to this real field does not affect any observable quantities nor their running, as had better be the case sinceϕ\phiorχ\chiis simply an auxiliary field. In fact, using the real response fieldϕ\phiis how MSRDJ is implemented from the start in[21,19], for example.

Proceeding in terms ofϕ\phihenceforth, we note that∇⋅𝐧^⟂\nabla\cdot\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}may also be written as−∂||ψ-\partial_{||}\psi, so that we can rewriteℰ=∂tψ+λ​∂||ψ−κ​∇2ψ,{\cal E}=\partial_{t}\psi+\lambda\partial_{||}\psi-\kappa\nabla^{2}\psi,(14)

andϕ​ℰ\displaystyle\phi{\cal E}=ϕ​[∂tψ+λ​(cos⁡ψ)​∂xψ+λ​(sin⁡ψ)​∂yψ−κ​∇2ψ]\displaystyle=\phi\bigl[\partial_{t}\psi+\lambda(\cos\psi)\partial_{x}\psi+\lambda(\sin\psi)\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\nabla^{2}\psi\bigr]=ϕ​[(∂t+λ​∂x−κ​∇2)​ψ+λ​(cos⁡ψ−1)​∂xψ+λ​(sin⁡ψ)​∂yψ].\displaystyle=\phi\bigl[(\partial_{t}+\lambda\partial_{x}-\kappa\nabla^{2})\psi+\lambda(\cos\psi-1)\partial_{x}\psi+\lambda(\sin\psi)\partial_{y}\psi\bigr].(15)

We can therefore decompose the Lagrangian into the following free and interaction parts:ℒfree\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm free}=ϕ​(∂t+λ​∂x−κ​∇2)​ψ−Δ​ϕ2,\displaystyle=\phi(\partial_{t}+\lambda\partial_{x}-\kappa\nabla^{2})\psi-\Delta\phi^{2},(16a)ℒint\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}=λ​ϕ​[(cos⁡ψ−1)​∂xψ+(sin⁡ψ)​∂yψ].\displaystyle=\lambda\phi\bigl[(\cos\psi-1)\partial_{x}\psi+(\sin\psi)\partial_{y}\psi\bigr].(16b)

Operationally, the distinction between free and interacting terms is in whether they consist of field bilinears, or higher order terms, respectively. The RG flow of this system may be analyzed using the standard techniques of perturbative field theory. The details of this computation are relegated to the appendices. For this purpose, however, it is simpler and more convenient to integrate by parts in the interaction Lagrangian and instead writeℒint=−λ​(sin⁡ψ−ψ)​∂xϕ+λ​(cos⁡ψ−1)​∂yϕ.\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}=-\lambda(\sin\psi-\psi)\partial_{x}\phi+\lambda(\cos\psi-1)\partial_{y}\phi.(17)

The Feynman rules are derived in AppendixA. The non-vanishing bare propagators are=⟨ψ−ω,𝐤​ψω,𝐤⟩0=2​Δ(ω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)​(ω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2),\displaystyle=\langle\psi_{-\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\,\psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}=\frac{2\Delta}{(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2})},(18a)=⟨ϕ−ω,−𝐤​ψω,𝐤⟩0=iω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2,\displaystyle=\langle\phi_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}\,\psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}=\frac{i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}},(18b)=⟨ψ−ω,−𝐤​ϕω,𝐤⟩0=−iω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2,\displaystyle=\langle\psi_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}\,\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}=\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}},(18c)

and the vertices are given as follows forn=1,2,3,…n=1,2,3,\ldots,V2​n+1=ω,𝐤2​n=(−1)n​i​λ​ky,V_{2n+1}=\vbox{\hbox{

}}=-(-1)^{n}i\lambda k_{x}.(20)

Note that there is a special relationship between the vertices, which derives from the fact that they come from the cosine and sine ofψ\psi:V2​n+1+ky​∂∂kx​V2​n+2\displaystyle V_{2n+1}+k_{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{x}}V_{2n+2}=0,\displaystyle=0,V2​n−kx​∂∂ky​V2​n+1\displaystyle V_{2n}-k_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{y}}V_{2n+1}=0.\displaystyle=0.(21)

It will turn out that this relation is preserved under renormalization as a result of the non-linear realization of a symmetry that ends up forcing relations between correlation functions at different orders, as elaborated upon in SectionIV.

Before detailing our findings, we ought to first understand the characteristic scales associated with the stochastic fluctuations and their dissipation. These are parameterized byΔ\Deltaandκ\kappa, respectively, as opposed to an overall pre-factor in front of the action in (7) that would play a role analogous to that played byℏ\hbarorβ−1\beta^{-1}in the context of quantum or thermal field theory. We could just as well have chosen to assign such an overall pre-factor with the dimensions of action, which would have consequently rescaled all couplings, and subsequently all anomalous dimensions generated by loop corrections. In analogous ‘natural’ units, this pre-factor would be given byΔ/κ\Delta/\kappa, which in the limit of vanishing interactions corresponds to the characteristic amplitude of the equal time Fourier mode fluctuations:k2​⟨|ψ​(t,k)|2⟩∼Δ/κk^{2}\langle|\psi(t,\textbf{k})|^{2}\rangle\sim\Delta/\kappa. Specifically, by taking theλ→0\lambda\to 0limit of (18a) and integrating over the frequency domain, one findsk2​⟨|ψ​(t,𝐤)|2⟩=k2​∫d​ω2​π​2​Δω2+κ2​k4=Δκ.k^{2}\langle|\psi(t,\mathbf{k})|^{2}\rangle=k^{2}\int\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}\frac{2\Delta}{\omega^{2}+\kappa^{2}k^{4}}=\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}.(22)

In what follows, we see how in spite of the fact thatκ\kappaandΔ\Deltaacquire anomalous dimensions, one can prove these to be identical to all loop orders. Therefore, it is the renormalization group invariant ratioΔ/κ\Delta/\kappathat sets the characteristic size of the fluctuations and determines the asymptotic behavior ofλ\lambda, and the fate of quasi-long-range order.

## IIIAll-Loop-Order Results

A combination of a non-renormalization theorem and a series of power-counting arguments discussed in detail in AppendixAprove that the only one-particle irreducible (1PI) diagrams that contribute either to propagator or vertex renormalizations are those with exactly one vertex, and any number ofψ\psi-ψ\psiloops attached to the vertex. }}+\cdots(24)

A single loop integral has a leading logarithmic divergence of the form (see app.B)ℐ=Δ4​π​κ​ln⁡Λμ,\mathscr{I}=\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\ln\frac{\Lambda}{\mu},(25)

whereΛ\Lambdaandμ\muare UV and IR cut-offs, respectively, although the same conclusions would have been arrived at in dimensional regularization, as also elaborated upon in the appendix.

The sum over all loops, whether in vertex or propagator renormalizations results in a uniform shift ofλ\lambdaas (cf. (167))λ→e−ℐ​λ=(Λμ)−Δ4​π​κ​λ.\lambda\rightarrow e^{-\mathscr{I}}\lambda=\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\lambda.(26)

In spite of first appearances, however, this shift is not absorbed entirely by the renormalization ofλ\lambda, but is instead distributed across the anomalous dimensions ofλ\lambda,Δ\Delta,κ\kappa, the fieldϕ\phi, and of the spatial coordinates relative to the time coordinate (i.e., the dynamical critical exponentzz), even as some of these parameters do not by themselves receive any explicit diagrammatic corrections. The latter feature of the renormalization procedure is specific to theories where boost invariance is absent, and is discussed in detail in AppendixD. Evidently,λ\lambdaends up absorbing the anomalous dimension(Λ/μ)−Δ2​π​κ(\Lambda/\mu)^{-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}, as opposed to(Λ/μ)−Δ4​π​κ(\Lambda/\mu)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}. Upon fixing renormalization conditions at some scaleμ¯\bar{\mu}, the running ofλ\lambdais given in (184) asλ​(μ)=(μ¯μ)1−Δ2​π​κ​λ​(μ¯).\lambda(\mu)=\biggl(\frac{\bar{\mu}}{\mu}\biggr)^{1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}\lambda(\bar{\mu}).(27)

This manifests the fact that the functional form of the cosine and sine derivative interactions are preserved to all orders under loop corrections, and therefore, the renormalized interactions are parameterized by a runningλ\lambda. The former can be traced to the non-linear realization of a symmetry that mixes global shifts ofψ\psicompensated by a spatial rotation, along with its associated Ward-Takahashi identities as detailed in the next section.

The net result is thatλ\lambda, which has a bare scaling dimension of 1, acquires an anomalous dimension:[λ]=1−Δ2​π​κ.[\lambda]=1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}.(28)

The dynamical critical exponent is reduced from its bare value of 2 to that in (183) given by555While we reach the same conclusion as[13]that the renormalized valued of the dynamical critical exponent isz<2z<2, we differ in how we arrive at it. Foremost, we find no diagrammatic corrections toκ\kappa, in contrast to the argument that these corrections are positive based upon an extrapolation from4−ε4-\varepsilondimensions, which evidently fails to extend all the way. We stress that the conclusion thatz<2z<2and the value given in (29) is exact to all orders.,z=21+Δ4​π​κ.z=\frac{2}{1+\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}.(29)

Similarly,κ\kappaandΔ\Delta, which have vanishing classical scaling dimension, acquire a negative anomalous dimension:[κ]=[Δ]=−Δ2​π​κ.[\kappa]=[\Delta]=-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}.(30)

Of course, this result implies that the ratioΔ/κ\Delta/\kappamust remain dimensionless even asΔ\Deltaandκ\kappagain anomalous dimensions. As we detail in the following section, both the universality of the renormalization ofλ\lambdaand the fact thatΔ\Delta,λ\lambda, andκ\kappaall acquire the same anomalous dimension are direct corollaries of the Ward-Takahashi identities associated to what nominally presents itself as a symmetry of the action, but is more accurately understood as a field redundancy of the MSRDJ formulation specific to the isotropic Malthusian flock.

We note in passing that were we to artificially consider deforming the diffusion operator to be anisotropic without introducing any additional derivatively coupled operators (cf. footnote15), so thatκx≠κy\kappa_{x}\neq\kappa_{y}. However, as discussed in AppendixA.1, it is still the case thatζ=1\zeta=1and thedimensionsofκx\kappa_{x}andκy\kappa_{y}are equal. One finds thatλ\lambdainstead acquires the anomalous dimension[λ]=1−Δ2​π​κx​κy.[\lambda]=1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\sqrt{\kappa_{x}\kappa_{y}}}.(31)

In this case,z\displaystyle z=21+Δ4​π​κx​κy,ζ=1,\displaystyle=\frac{2}{1+\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\sqrt{\kappa_{x}\kappa_{y}}}},\qquad\zeta=1,(32)

andΔ\Delta,κx\kappa_{x}, andκy\kappa_{y}get the anomalous dimensions:[Δ]\displaystyle[\Delta]=[κx]=[κy]=−Δ2​π​κx​κy.\displaystyle=[\kappa_{x}]=[\kappa_{y}]=-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\sqrt{\kappa_{x}\kappa_{y}}}.(33)

It is still the case that the ratioΔ/κx​κy\Delta/\sqrt{\kappa_{x}\kappa_{y}}remains dimensionless since[Δ/κx​κy]=0.[\Delta/\sqrt{\kappa_{x}\kappa_{y}}]=0.The extension to fully anisotropic diffusion is, of course, more involved and we address possible extensions of our results to this case in our concluding remarks.

Given that our results are exact to all-orders, there is no restriction on the value ofΔκ\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}other than positivity. Therefore, there is an entire line of fixed points parametrized by this ratio. This may come as a surprise given that bothΔ\Deltaandκ\kappagain negative anomalous dimensions, and are thus irrelevant. However, what matters is not their individual values, but only their ratioΔκ\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}, which remains exactly marginal throughout renormalization, and which sets the scale of stochastic fluctuations via (22). Compare this, for example, with the line of fixed points with a continuously varying dynamical critical exponent found in the eight-vertex model[26]. The line of fixed points corresponding to theU​(1)U(1)-symmetric quantum Lifshitz models has fixedz=2z=2, whereaszzvaries continuously along the line of fixed points with only discrete Isingℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}symmetry. The former falls under Model B (conserved order parameter) and the latter under Model A (no conservation laws) of the Halperin-Hohenberg classification of dynamic critical phenomena[22]. As with theℤ2\mathbb{Z}_{2}-symmetric case, our theory has a continuously varying dynamical critical exponent, and as discussed in SectionV.2, is best understood as being in a universality class that is analogous to the Berezinskii–Kosterlitz–Thouless class, albeit with important qualitative differences. The fact that our theory non-linearly realizes a rather special set of symmetries and redundancies underwrites its remarkable features. We elaborate on this in the next section.

## IVSymmetries and Ward-Takahashi Identities

That the recursion relations (21) for the vertex factors are preserved under renormalization turns out to be a consequence of a particular global symmetry and its associated Ward-Takahashi identities666Henceforth referred to simply as Ward identities, even though the truncated designation is typically used to refer to application of the Ward-Takahashi identities to transition matrix elements in the context of quantum field theory.. The symmetry in question is a subset of a larger set of invariances (whose elaboration will immediately follow), and whose transformations are given byδθ​ϕ\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}\phi=−i​θ​L​ϕ,\displaystyle=-i\theta L\phi,δθ​ψ\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}\psi=θ​(1−i​L​ψ),\displaystyle=\theta(1-iL\psi),(34)

whereLLis the angular momentum operator, which is the generator of rotations,L=−i​(x​∂y−y​∂x).L=-i(x\partial_{y}-y\partial_{x}).(35)

When computed not with respect to the Lagrangian in the form (8), but the one given by integration by parts:ℒ=−ψ​ℬ−Δ​ϕ2,{\cal L}=-\psi\mathscr{B}-\Delta\phi^{2},(36)

whereℬ\mathscr{B}is theψ\psiequation of motion given in (13), the Noether current associated with the above symmetry isJt\displaystyle J^{t}=i​ψ​L​ϕ+ϕ,\displaystyle=i\psi L\phi+\phi,(37a)𝐉\displaystyle{\mathbf{J}}=(λ​𝐧^​ϕ+D​∇ϕ)​(1−i​L​ψ)−D​ϕ​∇(−i​L​ψ)+Δ​ϕ2​(−yx)+D​∇ϕ.\displaystyle=(\lambda\hat{{\mathbf{n}}}\phi+D\nabla\phi)(1-iL\psi)-D\phi\nabla(-iL\psi)+\Delta\phi^{2}\binom{-y}{x}+D\nabla\phi.(37b)

The divergence of this current is∂μJμ=i​𝒜​L​ϕ+ℬ​(1−i​L​ψ),\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}=i\mathscr{A}L\phi+\mathscr{B}(1-iL\psi),(38)

which vanishes on-shell since𝒜=0\mathscr{A}=0andℬ=0\mathscr{B}=0are the equations of motion.

For concreteness, let us consider the Ward identity applied to the four-point vertex operator:ℱ=∫t,𝐱ϕ​(t,𝐱)​ψ​(t,𝐱)3.{\cal F}=\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}})\,\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{3}.(39)

for which the corresponding Ward identity reads⟨δθ​ℱ⟩=θ​∫t′,𝐱′⟨ℱ​∂μJμ​(t′,𝐱′)⟩.\langle\delta_{\theta}{\cal F}\rangle=\theta\int_{t^{\prime},{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime}}\langle{\cal F}\,\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}(t^{\prime},{\mathbf{x}}^{\prime})\rangle.(40)

As detailed in AppendixA, the expectation value of any operator with two externalϕ\phifields vanishes. Sinceℱ{\cal F}contains one factor ofϕ\phiand every term in∂μJμ\partial_{\mu}J^{\mu}contains one factor ofϕ\phi, it follows that the right hand side of the Ward identity vanishes. Hence,⟨δθ​ℱ⟩=0.\langle\delta_{\theta}{\cal F}\rangle=0.(41)

Now, in momentum space, the transformations (34) read777Where in keeping with the conventions of AppendixA,δ​¯​(ω):=δ​(ω)/(2​π)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega):=\delta(\omega)/(2\pi), andδ​¯(d)​(k):=δ​(k)/(2​π)d\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{\,(d)}(\textbf{k}):=\delta(\textbf{k})/(2\pi)^{d}.δθ​ϕω,𝐤\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}=−i​θ​L~​ϕω,𝐤,\displaystyle=-i\theta\widetilde{L}\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}},δθ​ψω,𝐤\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}\psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}=−i​θ​L~​ψω,𝐤+θ​δ​¯​(ω)​δ​¯(d)​(𝐤),\displaystyle=-i\theta\widetilde{L}\psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}+\theta\,\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt(\omega)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{\,(d)}({\mathbf{k}}),(42)

whereL~=−i​(ky​∂∂kx−kx​∂∂ky).\widetilde{L}=-i\biggl(k_{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{x}}-k_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{y}}\biggr).(43)

Meanwhile,ℱ{\cal F}readsℱ=(∏i=13∫ωi,𝐤iψωi,𝐤i)​∫ω4,𝐤4ϕω4,𝐤4​δ​¯​(∑i=14ωi)​δ​¯(d)​(∑i=14𝐤i).{\cal F}=\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{3}\int_{\omega_{i},{\mathbf{k}}_{i}}\psi_{\omega_{i},{\mathbf{k}}_{i}}\biggr)\int_{\omega_{4},{\mathbf{k}}_{4}}\phi_{\omega_{4},{\mathbf{k}}_{4}}\,\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\omega_{i}\biggr)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{\,(d)}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}{\mathbf{k}}_{i}\biggr).(44)

Therefore,δθ​ℱ\displaystyle\delta_{\theta}{\cal F}=θ​(∏i=14∫ωi,𝐤i)​(∑i=14(−i​L~i))​(∏i=13ψωi,𝐤i)​ϕω4,𝐤4​δ​¯​(∑i=14ωi)​δ​¯(d)​(∑i=14𝐤i)\displaystyle=\theta\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{4}\int_{\omega_{i},{\mathbf{k}}_{i}}\biggr)\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}(-i\widetilde{L}_{i})\biggr)\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{3}\psi_{\omega_{i},{\mathbf{k}}_{i}}\biggr)\phi_{\omega_{4},{\mathbf{k}}_{4}}\,\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}\omega_{i}\biggr)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{\,(d)}\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{4}{\mathbf{k}}_{i}\biggr)+θ​∑i=13(∏j≠i3∫ωj,𝐤jψωj,𝐤j)​∫ω4,𝐤4ϕω4,𝐤4​δ​¯​(∑j≠i4ωj)​δ​¯(d)​(∑j≠i4𝐤j).\displaystyle\quad+\theta\sum_{i=1}^{3}\biggl(\prod_{j\neq i}^{3}\int_{\omega_{j},{\mathbf{k}}_{j}}\psi_{\omega_{j},{\mathbf{k}}_{j}}\biggr)\int_{\omega_{4},{\mathbf{k}}_{4}}\phi_{\omega_{4},{\mathbf{k}}_{4}}\,\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\biggl(\sum_{j\neq i}^{4}\omega_{j}\biggr)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{\,(d)}\biggl(\sum_{j\neq i}^{4}{\mathbf{k}}_{j}\biggr).(45)

Once the expectation value is taken, the first line turns into the operator−i​L~-i\widetilde{L}, whereL~\widetilde{L}is the total sum of the fourL~i\widetilde{L}_{i}’s, acting on the vertexV4V_{4}, while the second line turns into three copies ofV3V_{3}. SinceV4V_{4}only depends on the momentum of the externalψ\psifield being contracted with the internalϕ\phifield, the factorL~4\widetilde{L}_{4}does not contribute at all. Meanwhile, sinceV4V_{4}depends only onkxk_{x}, the term−kx​∂∂ky-k_{x}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{y}}inL~\widetilde{L}does not contribute either, only the termky​∂∂kxk_{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{x}}does. Thus, the Ward identity simplifies intoV3+ky​∂∂kx​V4=0.V_{3}+k_{y}\frac{\partial}{\partial k_{x}}V_{4}=0.(46)

This relation holds at all orders, and therefore for the fully dressed vertex factors. In other words, the relation (21) for the bare vertices is satisfied at all loop orders. If we letℱ{\cal F}be an odd-point function, then we will derive the other corresponding relation in (21). The Ward identity ensures that these recursion relations are preserved under renormalization.

The symmetry (34) corresponds to a constant shift in the flock angle fieldψ\psi, along with a compensating rigid rotation of the underlying coordinates by the same angle. It seems obvious that this would have to be a symmetry of the action given that it relates indistinguishable physical configurations, provided the functional measure is also invariant under this transformation, as can be straightforwardly verified.

However, the isotropic Malthusian flock exhibits an even larger invariance. To see this, we first rewrite the MSRDJ action so that a special structural property becomes evident. By first noting thatℰ\mathcal{E}in (4) can be written asℰ\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=∂μjψμ,\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}j_{\psi}^{\mu},jψt\displaystyle j^{t}_{\psi}=ψ,\displaystyle=\psi,𝐣ψ\displaystyle\mathbf{j}_{\psi}=−κ​∇ψ−λ​𝐧^⟂,\displaystyle=-\kappa\nabla\psi-\lambda\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp},(47)

we see that the Lagrangian takes the form:ℒ=ϕ​∂μjψμ−Δ​ϕ2.{\cal L}=\phi\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}-\Delta\phi^{2}.(48)

Integrating by parts results inℒ=−∂μϕ​jψμ−Δ​ϕ2,{\cal L}=-\partial_{\mu}\phi\,j^{\mu}_{\psi}-\Delta\phi^{2},(49)

which highlights the fact that the currentjψμj^{\mu}_{\psi}couples only to derivatives of the response field. Therefore it behooves us to consider the set of all possible redundancies of field configurations that derive from the MSRDJ action that result in the same physical configuration under constant shifts of the response field. We do this in AppendixE, where we examine how the Lagrangian transforms under a constant shift of the response field along with coordinate transformations, such thatϕ​(x)→ϕ′​(x′)=ϕ​(x)+c,xμ→x′⁣μ=xμ+ϵμ​(x),\phi(x)\to\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\phi(x)+c,\qquad x^{\mu}\to x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}(x),(50)

where both transformations are considered to be infinitessimal, along with the yet to be determined transformations ofψ​(x)→ψ′​(x′)\psi(x)\to\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})which would result in a transformation of the current of the form:jψμ​(x)→jψ′′⁣μ​(x′)=∂x′⁣μ∂xν​jψν​(x)+ωμ​(x),j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)\rightarrow j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\partial x^{\prime\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}j^{\nu}_{\psi}(x)+\omega^{\mu}(x),(51)

so that the Lagrangian transforms asℒ→ℒ+c​∂μjψμ+ϕ​(∂μωμ+∂2ϵν∂xν​∂xμ​jψμ​(x)−2​Δ​c).\mathcal{L}\to\mathcal{L}+c\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}+\phi\biggl(\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j_{\psi}^{\mu}(x)-2\Delta c\biggr).(52)

Therefore, the action is invariant under the transformations (50) ifωμ\omega^{\mu}satisfies∂μωμ=2​Δ​c−(∂μ∂νϵν)​jψμ.\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=2\Delta c-(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon^{\nu})j^{\mu}_{\psi}.(53)

Given thatωμ\omega^{\mu}implicitly depends onψ′\psi^{\prime}via the analogue of (47) and the transformation (51), we see that (53) represents an implicit equation for the transformationψ→ψ′\psi\to\psi^{\prime}such that the action is invariant if it is satisfied. That is, given a particular set of transformations (50), one can impose (53) todeterminethe transformed field configurationψ′​(x′)\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})such that the action remains invariant, and therefore corresponds to an identical physical configuration.

For illustrative purposes, we consider the coordinate transformation given by an anti-clockwise rotation by a spatially-independent but time dependent angleθ​(t)\theta(t):t′\displaystyle t^{\prime}=t,\displaystyle=t,x′\displaystyle x^{\prime}=x−y​θ,\displaystyle=x-y\theta,y′\displaystyle y^{\prime}=y+x​θ.\displaystyle=y+x\theta.(54)

Given that∂μϵμ=0\partial_{\mu}\epsilon^{\mu}=0for this transformation, the condition (192) becomes simply∂μωμ=2​Δ​c\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=2\Delta c. As show in AppendixE, via equations (194a) - (194c) thatδ​ψ≡ψ′​(x′)−ψ​(x)=jψ′′⁣t−jψt=ωt,\delta\psi\equiv\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)=j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime t}-j^{t}_{\psi}=\omega^{t},(55)

eventually results in the following condition forW≡ωtW\equiv\omega^{t}W˙−κ​∇2W+λ​n^​(ψ)⋅∇W+λ​W​∇⋅n^​(ψ)=2​Δ​c+i​θ˙​L​ψ+λ​θ​∇⋅n^​(ψ),\dot{W}-\kappa\nabla^{2}W+\lambda\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla W+\lambda W\nabla\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi)=2\Delta c+i\dot{\theta}L\psi+\lambda\theta\nabla\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi),(56)

such that ifWWis a solution to the above, then the condition∂μωμ=2​Δ​c\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=2\Delta cwill be satisfied. Since Eq. (56) is a first-order convection–diffusion equation, standard existence and uniqueness results imply that, for any specified boundary conditions, unique solutions can be obtained for sufficiently continuous background field (i.e.ψ\psi) evolution[3,32]. Consequently, given a constant shift of the response field,ϕ→ϕ+c\phi\rightarrow\phi+ctogether with an infinitesimal time-dependent rotation, there always exists a unique functionW​(t,x,y)W(t,x,y)solving Eq. (56),such that the transformationψ→ψ+W​(t,x,y)\psi\to\psi+W(t,x,y)(57)

leaves the MSRDJ action invariant. The diagonal rotation/shift symmetry is obtained by combining a constant rotation with a constant shift of the flock angle. Settingc=0c=0,θ​(t)=ν\theta(t)=\nu, andW=νW=\nu, one verifies directly that Eq. (56) is satisfied. More generally, takingθ=0\theta=0implies that for every constant shiftϕ→ϕ+c\phi\rightarrow\phi+c, there exists a corresponding compensating transformationψ→ψ+W\psi\rightarrow\psi+Wwhich preserves the action. Configurations related by such transformations therefore represent the same stochastic dynamics. The construction can be extended further by considering more general spacetime-dependent transformations and solving Eq. (56) for the compensating fieldWW, thereby defining the induced transformation of the flock angle throughδ​ψ≡W\delta\psi\equiv W.

At first sight, the existence of a solution for each admissible transformation may suggest the presence of an extensive symmetry group. Such an interpretation, however, reflects intuition developed for Hamiltonian field theories and is misleading in the dissipative, nonequilibrium setting described by the MSRDJ formalism. Rather, the special structure of the isotropic Malthusian flock reveals a non-local, field-dependent redundancy of the MSRDJ representation. This redundancy is generated by constant shifts of the response field together with compensating deformations of the matter field determined by Eq. (56). As a result, the space of physically inequivalent MSRDJ field configurations acquires a quotient structure, and the stochastic dynamics is naturally defined on the physical configuration spaceℳphys={ψ,ϕ}{(ψ,ϕ)∼(ψ+W,ϕ+c)}.\mathcal{M}_{\mathrm{phys}}=\frac{\{\psi,\phi\}}{\{(\psi,\phi)\sim(\psi+W,\phi+c)\}}.(58)

Although this structure is reminiscent of a gauge redundancy, the transformation is implemented through the Green’s function of a drift–diffusion operator whose coefficients depend nonlinearly onψ\psi. It therefore does not constitute a conventional local gauge invariance. Instead, it should be understood as a non-local, field-dependent redundancy—or equivalence relation—within the MSRDJ description. Its primary significance is that it provides additional constraints among renormalization factors and anomalous dimensions, supplying the relations required to complete the renormalization program for the isotropic Malthusian flock.

Consider just the transformation generated byccwith no associated coordinate transformation:ϕ′=ϕ+c\phi^{\prime}=\phi+candψ′=ψ+Wc\psi^{\prime}=\psi+W_{c}whereWcW_{c}solves Eq. (56) withθ=0\theta=0. The Noether current is simplyjμj^{\mu}and the Ward identity acting on a product of an arbitrary number ofψ\psi’s with oneϕ\phireads(∂∂t−κ​∇x2)​⟨ψ​(x)​(∏i=1nψ​(yi))​ϕ​(z)⟩−λ​∇x⋅⟨𝐧^⟂​[ψ​(x)]​(∏i=1nψ​(yi))​ϕ​(z)⟩\displaystyle\biggl(\frac{\partial}{\partial t}-\kappa\nabla_{x}^{2}\biggr)\biggl\langle\psi(x)\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi(y_{i})\biggr)\phi(z)\biggr\rangle-\lambda\nabla_{x}\cdot\biggl\langle\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}[\psi(x)]\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi(y_{i})\biggr)\phi(z)\biggr\rangle(59)=1c​∑i=1nδ​(x−yi)​⟨Wc​(yi)​(∏j≠inψ​(yj))​ϕ​(z)⟩+δ​(x−z)​⟨∏i=1nψ​(yi)⟩.\displaystyle=\frac{1}{c}\sum_{i=1}^{n}\delta(x-y_{i})\biggl\langle W_{c}(y_{i})\biggl(\prod_{j\neq i}^{n}\psi(y_{j})\biggr)\phi(z)\biggr\rangle+\delta(x-z)\biggl\langle\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi(y_{i})\biggr\rangle.

Note that then=0n=0case with𝐧^⟂\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}expanded to linear order inψ\psiis simply the fundamental equation satisfied by the propagator⟨ψ​(x)​ϕ​(z)⟩\langle\psi(x)\phi(z)\rangle. In general, this identity depends on all three parametersλ\lambda,Δ\Delta, andκ\kappathroughWcW_{c}.

Now, consider the transformation withc=0c=0andθ=α​t\theta=\alpha t. Then, the variations of the fields readδ​ϕ=−i​α​t​L​ϕ\delta\phi=-i\alpha tL\phiandδ​ψ=−i​α​t​L​ψ+Wα\delta\psi=-i\alpha tL\psi+W_{\alpha}whereWαW_{\alpha}solves Eq. (56) withc=0c=0andθ=α​t\theta=\alpha t. The Noether current isJαt=0J_{\alpha}^{t}=0,Jαx=ℒ​t​yJ_{\alpha}^{x}={\cal L}ty, andJαy=−ℒ​t​xJ_{\alpha}^{y}=-{\cal L}tx. Since every term inℒ{\cal L}contains at least oneϕ\phi, the functional that we are inserting in the Ward identity contains exactly oneϕ\phi, and an expectation value containing two or moreϕ\phi’s necessarily vanishes, the Ward identity reads∑i=1n⟨Wα​(yi)​(∏j≠inψ​(yj))​ϕ​(z)⟩=i​α​(∑i=1nti​Lyi+tz​Lz)​⟨(∏i=1nψ​(yi))​ϕ​(z)⟩,\sum_{i=1}^{n}\biggl\langle W_{\alpha}(y_{i})\biggl(\prod_{j\neq i}^{n}\psi(y_{j})\biggr)\phi(z)\biggr\rangle=i\alpha\biggl(\sum_{i=1}^{n}t_{i}L_{y_{i}}+t_{z}L_{z}\biggr)\biggl\langle\biggl(\prod_{i=1}^{n}\psi(y_{i})\biggr)\phi(z)\biggr\rangle,(60)

wheretit_{i}is the time coordinate ofyiy_{i}andtzt_{z}is the time coordinate ofzz. This Ward identity depends onλ\lambdaandκ\kappa, but not onΔ\Delta. Note that this reduces to the case of a constant rotation by simply removing thettfactors and settingWα=αW_{\alpha}=\alpha, which is indeed the solution to Eq. (56) in the caseθ=α=const\theta=\alpha=\text{const}. In this case, the dependence onκ\kappadrops out and the Ward identity protects only the cosine and sine forms of the interaction terms in the convective derivative, as previously shown. These Ward identities imply cancellations between expectation values involving subsets of the three parametersλ\lambda,Δ\Delta, andκ\kappa, which must be preserved under renormalization, implying that the anomalous dimensions of these three parameters must be the same.

The transformations discussed above are of course, supplemented with spacetime translation symmetries, whose associated Ward identities simply force correlation functions to depend on differences of space and time coordinates. We note for completeness that the Noether charge associated with time translation invariance does not correspond to a conserved energy within the MSRDJ formalism per se, rather, the conserved generator of the Hamiltonian flow underlying the saddle-point equations. On the other hand, spatial translation invariance does indeed imply the conservation of linear momentum in inter-flock interactions (even if momentum is not conserved in flock-reservoir interactions – a generic feature of systems where boost invariance is kinematically absent[14,20]). Operationally, it is the Ward identities associated with the diagonal subgroup parameterized byθ\thetathat results in the renormalizability of the isotropic Malthusian flock model: The sine and cosine interaction potentials preserve their functional form under renormalization group improvement, up to an overall scale dependent rescaling that gets encoded into a runningλ\lambda. This is a highly non-trivial result, which need not have followed directly from the known power counting renormalizability of allz=2z=2statistical field theories in2+12+1-dimensions with fewer than four derivatives, but is rather a consequence of a non-linearly realized symmetry that forces relations between correlation functions of different orders888That is, given that (34) corresponds to transformations of the formδθ​ψ=θ+\delta_{\theta}\psi=\theta+(rotation), it immediately follows thatδθ​⟨ψ​(t1,x1)​…​ψ​(tn,xn)⟩\delta_{\theta}\langle\psi(t_{1},\textbf{x}_{1})...\psi(t_{n},\textbf{x}_{n})\ranglereturns a sum of(n−1)(n-1)-pt correlation functions plus rotatednn-point functions, which is the content of (IV), with the eventual corollary (46)..

## VThe Fate of Quasi-Long-Range Order

In this section, we address the nature and phase structure of quasi-long-range order in isotropic Mathusian flocks. We do so by directly examining the relevant limits of the renormalized propagators of the theory, after which we speculate on the physical principles behind our results given the symmetries and invariances discussed in the previous section.

We begin by noting that at weak coupling, the existence and nature of long-range order can be inferred from the static, low-momentum limit of the renormalizedψ\psi-ψ\psipropagator:𝒢2,0ren:=𝒢ψ​ψren=2​Δ(ω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)​(ω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2),{\cal G}_{2,0}^{\rm ren}:={\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}=\frac{2\Delta}{(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2})},(61)

where all the parameters above now stand for their renormalized versions. Although two-point correlation functions no longer suffice in completely determining the nature of the long-range correlations between the collective excitations that will have condensed in any strongly coupled regime, they can still relay valuable information about the nature of this phase. Consequently, we consider the static limit of the above for all values ofλ\lambda, by sendingω→0\omega\to 0:𝒢ψ​ψren→ω=02​Δλ2​(𝐤⋅𝐤^b)2+κ2​k4,{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}\xrightarrow{\omega=0}\frac{2\Delta}{\lambda^{2}({\mathbf{k}}\cdot\widehat{{\mathbf{k}}}_{b})^{2}+\kappa^{2}k^{4}},(62)

where we have explicitly restored the implicit background field dependence in the propagator, with𝐤^b\widehat{{\mathbf{k}}}_{b}denoting the unit vector along the net flock direction, which we have assumed without loss of generality taken to point along thexx-axis. Consider now the behavior of the above staticstructure factorin the two cases: (1) as a function ofkxk_{x}whenky=0k_{y}=0; and (2) as a function ofkyk_{y}whenkx=0k_{x}=0. In case (1), if the function has the same scaling dimension as1kx2\frac{1}{k_{x}^{2}}in the limit thatkx→0k_{x}\rightarrow 0, we can infer the existence of quasi-long-range order in thexx-direction. This describes a two-point function in real space that decays exponentially in thexxdirection with some characteristic correlation lengthξx\xi_{x}in the limit in whichξx→∞\xi_{x}\rightarrow\infty. This follows from the fact that the Fourier transform ofe−x/ξxe^{-x/\xi_{x}}alongxxis given by2(1/ξx)2+kx2\frac{2}{(1/\xi_{x})^{2}+k_{x}^{2}}, which goes to2kx2\frac{2}{k_{x}^{2}}in the limitξx→∞\xi_{x}\rightarrow\infty. The same statement holds for case (2) withkxk_{x}replaced withkyk_{y}.

More precisely, we are interested in the asymptotic behavior of the static real space correlation function either in the limit thatlim|x|→∞⟨ψ​(x,y)​ψ​(0,0)⟩∼|x|−ηx​ℱ​(y|x|),\lim_{|x|\to\infty}\langle\psi(x,y)\psi(0,0)\rangle\sim|x|^{-\eta_{x}}\mathcal{F}\left(\frac{y}{|x|}\right),(63)

where the ratioy/|x|y/|x|is kept fixed, or in the limitlim|y|→∞⟨ψ​(x,y)​ψ​(0,0)⟩∼|y|−ηy​𝒦​(x|y|)\lim_{|y|\to\infty}\langle\psi(x,y)\psi(0,0)\rangle\sim|y|^{-\eta_{y}}\mathcal{K}\left(\frac{x}{|y|}\right)(64)

where the ratiox/|y|x/|y|is kept fixed. Quasi-long-range order along thexx-axis requiresηx>0\eta_{x}>0, and similarly,ηy>0\eta_{y}>0if it is to exist along theyy-axis, which is the direction perpendicular to the flock in terms of our background field expansion. The translation of these statements into Fourier space imply that by taking the limit of the renormalized propagator𝒢ψ​ψren​(ω,kx,ky)|ω,ky=0∼1|kx|2−ηx≡1|kx|Zx,case​(1){\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}(\omega,k_{x},k_{y})\big|_{\omega,k_{y}=0}\sim\frac{1}{|k_{x}|^{2-\eta_{x}}}\equiv\frac{1}{|k_{x}|^{Z_{x}}},\qquad{\rm case~}(1)(65)

one has quasi-long-range order along thexx-direction if the scaling exponentZx<2Z_{x}<2. Similarly, one has quasi-long-range order along theyy-direction if in the corresponding limit𝒢ψ​ψren​(ω,kx,ky)|ω,kx=0∼1|ky|2−ηy≡1|kx|Zy,case​(2){\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}(\omega,k_{x},k_{y})\big|_{\omega,k_{x}=0}\sim\frac{1}{|k_{y}|^{2-\eta_{y}}}\equiv\frac{1}{|k_{x}|^{Z_{y}}},\qquad{\rm case~}(2)(66)

one also findsZy<2Z_{y}<2. For case (1), one finds that along thexx-direction that𝒢ψ​ψren→ω=ky=02​Δλ2​kx2.{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}\xrightarrow{\omega=k_{y}=0}\frac{2\Delta}{\lambda^{2}k_{x}^{2}}.(67)

Stripping off the bare dimensions of the coefficient2​Δλ2\frac{2\Delta}{\lambda^{2}}of1kx2\frac{1}{k_{x}^{2}}just gives the anomalous dimension of this coefficient, namelyγΔ−2​γλ=−γλ=Δ2​π​κ\gamma_{\Delta}-2\gamma_{\lambda}=-\gamma_{\lambda}=\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}, where the anomalous dimensions are given in (182). Factoring in the dimension of1kx2\frac{1}{k_{x}^{2}}simply gives−2​[kx]+Δ2​π​κ-2[k_{x}]+\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}. Measuring this in units of[kx][k_{x}]gives−2​[kx]+Δ2​π​κ[kx]=−2+Δ2​π​κ1+Δ4​π​κ=−21+Δ4​π​κ=−z,\frac{-2[k_{x}]+\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}{[k_{x}]}=-2+\frac{\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}{1+\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}=-\frac{2}{1+\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}=-z,(68)

wherezzis given by (183), so thatZx≡z.Z_{x}\equiv z.(69)

In other words, the static structure factor effectively decays in thexxdirection as1kxz\frac{1}{k_{x}^{z}}. Sincez<2z<2, this implies that the necessary condition for the existence of quasi-long-range order along the flocking direction is met. However, this is not a sufficient condition, since we furthermore observe that the dimension ofλ\lambdais1−Δ2​π​κ1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}, and so there is a critical ratio(Δκ)c=2​π,\biggl(\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}\biggr)_{c}=2\pi,(70)

whereλ\lambdais irrelevant ifΔκ>(Δκ)c\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}>\bigl(\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}\bigr)_{c}. Above this critical value, at long wavelengths, the system effectively has no drift term, only diffusion and noise. That system will therefore have no quasi-long-range order.

On the other hand, along theyy-axis, one finds instead that𝒢ψ​ψren→ω=kx=02​Δκ2​ky4,{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}\xrightarrow{\omega=k_{x}=0}\frac{2\Delta}{\kappa^{2}k_{y}^{4}},(71)

which has scaling dimensionZy=4+Δ2​π​κZ_{y}=4+\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}, and so there is no possibility for any ordered correlations along theyy-axis, consistent with our assumption that this is the direction perpendicular to the flock.

We can understand these results intuitively as follows: quasi-long-range order should tend to be destroyed by increasing the noiseΔ\Deltaor decreasing diffusionκ\kappa. Increasing noise will tend to randomizeψ\psifrom point to point while decreasing diffusion inhibits information flow or communication through the system. Both effects should tend to destroy quasi-long-range order. In the linearized theory, increasingΔ/κ\Delta/\kappaenhances fluctuations but does not change their logarithmic scaling, so quasi–long-range order persists for any finite ratio. However, in the full nonlinear theory, largeΔ/κ\Delta/\kappadrives the system into a strong-coupling regime where nonlinear effects enhance fluctuations beyond logarithmic growth, thereby destroying the possibility of quasi–long-range order. WhenΔ/κ\Delta/\kappais below the critical value, RG flow to the IR implies finite values forλ\lambdaat finite separation, with quasi-long-range order along the flocking direction indicated though the IR asymptotics of the fully renormalized propagator.

## V.1Soft limits and symmetry-protected gapless excitations

Perhaps the most interesting aspect about the isotropic Malthusian flock, is that many of its properties follow directly from the fact that its fields non-linearly realize an exact diagonal rotation/shift symmetry, and inherits a large set of field space redundancies from the special form of its MSRDJ action. These properties include the fact that the advection terms corresponding to the sine and cosine derivative interactions preserve their functional form under RG improvement, and that the couplingsλ\lambda,Δ\Delta, andκ\kappaare forced to have the same anomalous dimensions as a result of the Ward identities discussed in SectionIV. Factoring certain additional subtleties that arise in the boost non-invariant context, one furthermore infers that the absence of diagrammatic corrections to a given coupling does not preclude them from acquiring an anomalous dimension, as discussed in AppendixD. It remains to elaborate on perhaps the most significant corollary of diagonal shift symmetry as far as the present study is concerned: The fact that the gapless excitations are protected by this symmetry. The gapless property is a necessary condition for correlation functions to have the requisite IR scaling for quasi-long-range order, since gapped excitations decay exponentially at long wavelengths.

We proceed by first establishing how the Ward identities satisfied by the effective action imply soft identities for its functional derivatives999Soft theorems relatenn-point momentum space correlation functions where one of the external momenta is taken to be vanishing, with linear combinations of (possibly symmetry transformed) lower point correlators[Cheung:2014dqa,Cheung:2016drk]., from which the existence of symmetry-protected gapless excitations follow as a corollary. We first recall the fact that the MSRDJ action is invariant under the action of the symmetry defined in (34). Defining the generating functionalZ​[Jψ,Jϕ]=∫D​ψ​D​ϕ​exp⁡[−S​[ψ,ϕ]+∫d3​x​(Jψ​ψ+Jϕ​ϕ)],Z[J_{\psi},J_{\phi}]=\int D\psi\,D\phi\,\exp\left[-S[\psi,\phi]+\int d^{3}x\,\left(J_{\psi}\psi+J_{\phi}\phi\right)\right],(72)

whereJψJ_{\psi}andJϕJ_{\phi}are independent sources, and using the fact that the action and measure are invariant under the diagonal rotation/shift symmetry, one obtains the functional identity∫d3​x​[Jψ​(1−i​L​δ​Wδ​Jψ)−i​Jϕ​L​δ​Wδ​Jϕ]=0,\int d^{3}x\,\left[J_{\psi}\left(1-iL\frac{\delta W}{\delta J_{\psi}}\right)-iJ_{\phi}L\frac{\delta W}{\delta J_{\phi}}\right]=0,(73)

whereW=ln⁡ZW=\ln Z. Legenndre transforming to the effective action,Γ​[ψ¯,ϕ¯]=W−∫d3​x​(Jψ​ψ¯+Jϕ​ϕ¯),\Gamma[\bar{\psi},\bar{\phi}]=W-\int d^{3}x\,\left(J_{\psi}\bar{\psi}+J_{\phi}\bar{\phi}\right),(74)

where we’ve defined the background fieldsψ¯=δ​W/δ​Jψ\bar{\psi}=\delta W/\delta J_{\psi}andϕ¯=δ​W/δ​Jϕ\bar{\phi}=\delta W/\delta J_{\phi}and using the fact thatJψ=−δ​Γ/δ​ψ¯J_{\psi}=-\delta\Gamma/\delta\bar{\psi}, andJϕ=−δ​Γ/δ​ϕ¯J_{\phi}=-{\delta\Gamma}/{\delta\bar{\phi}}, the Ward identity becomes∫d3​x​[δ​Γδ​ψ¯​(1−i​L​ψ¯)−δ​Γδ​ϕ¯​i​L​ϕ¯]=0.\int d^{3}x\,\left[\frac{\delta\Gamma}{\delta\bar{\psi}}\left(1-iL\bar{\psi}\right)-\frac{\delta\Gamma}{\delta\bar{\phi}}iL\bar{\phi}\right]=0.(75)

This identity directly constrains the one-particle-irreducible vertices.
To extract the zero-momentum two-point vertex, differentiate
Eq. (75) once with respect toψ¯​(y)\bar{\psi}(y)and
then evaluate at a homogeneous background,ψ¯=ψ0,ϕ¯=0.\bar{\psi}=\psi_{0},\qquad\bar{\phi}=0.(76)

SinceLz​ψ0=0L_{z}\psi_{0}=0, one obtains∫d3​x​Γψ​ψ(2)​(x,y)=0.\int d^{3}x\,\Gamma^{(2)}_{\psi\psi}(x,y)=0.(77)

By translation invariance,Γψ​ψ(2)​(x,y)=Γψ​ψ(2)​(x−y),\Gamma^{(2)}_{\psi\psi}(x,y)=\Gamma^{(2)}_{\psi\psi}(x-y),(78)

so that finallyΓψ​ψ(2)​(ω=0,𝐪=𝟎)=0.\Gamma^{(2)}_{\psi\psi}(\omega=0,\mathbf{q}=\mathbf{0})=0.(79)

The Ward identity therefore implies a soft theorem associated with the generator of the exact diagonal rotational symmetry, which is spontaneously broken in the ordered phase. In particular, the inverse propagator vanishes at zero frequency and momentumΓψ​ψ(2)​(0,0)=0\Gamma_{\psi\psi}^{(2)}(0,0)=0, guaranteeing a gapless flock angle mode. Equivalently, any local potential or mass term would violate Eq. (75), because the constant part of the transformation shifts the homogeneous mode ofψ\psi. The effective action may therefore depend onψ\psionly through symmetry-compatible derivative and rotationally locked structures, as is implicit in the form of the action of the isotropic Malthusian flock. Consequently,ψ\psiremains gapless to all orders in perturbation theory101010This protection ultimately relies only on the exact diagonal rotation/shift symmetry of the interacting action. It did not require an Adler-zero soft theorem associated with the incorrectly identified Galileon invariance in a previous version of our paper..

## V.2A line of marginal vertex instabilities

The renormalization group running described by (27) implies that the IR behavior of the isotropic Malthusian flock is determined by a marginal instability across a line of fixed points, rather than by an isolated interacting fixed point of the Wilson–Fisher type. That is, given thatλ\lambdaruns asλ​(μ)=(μ¯μ)1−Δ2​π​κ​λ​(μ¯),\lambda(\mu)=\biggl(\frac{\bar{\mu}}{\mu}\biggr)^{1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}\lambda(\bar{\mu}),(80)

we see that the infrared scaling dimension of the nonlinear coupling flips sign at the critical ratioΔκ=2​π.\frac{\Delta}{\kappa}=2\pi.(81)

Sinceμ\muis an energy scale, the infrared limit corresponds toμ→0\mu\rightarrow 0, such that forΔ/κ<2​π\Delta/\kappa<2\pithe nonlinear coupling grows under coarse graining and is therefore infrared relevant for any finite separation, possibly crossing into a strong coupling regime. Conversely, forΔ/κ>2​π\Delta/\kappa>2\pi, the coupling flows to zero and is thus irrelevant in the IR. The coupling is exactly marginal at the critical value (81), and the resulting phase diagram is therefore characterized by a line of fixed points with a marginal instability separating two distinct regimes, with a disordered phase on one side, and a potentially strongly coupled gapless phase on the other, where moreover, quasi-long-range order persists.

In order to better understand the nature of the transition, it is useful to rewrite the derivatively coupled advection potential (16b) in terms of so-calledvertex operators. That is, we rewriteℒint=λ​ϕ​[(cos⁡ψ−1)​∂xψ+(sin⁡ψ)​∂yψ],\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=\lambda\phi\left[(\cos\psi-1)\partial_{x}\psi+(\sin\psi)\partial_{y}\psi\right],(82)

asℒint=λ2​ϕ​[(ei​ψ+e−i​ψ−2)​∂xψ−i​(ei​ψ−e−i​ψ)​∂yψ],\mathcal{L}_{\rm int}=\frac{\lambda}{2}\phi\Big[\left(e^{i\psi}+e^{-i\psi}-2\right)\partial_{x}\psi-i\left(e^{i\psi}-e^{-i\psi}\right)\partial_{y}\psi\Big],(83)

so that the nonlinear interactions can be constructed as a combination of vertex operatorsV±=e±i​ψ,V_{\pm}=e^{\pm i\psi},(84)

with derivative self couplings111111Or equivalently, derivatively coupled to the response field upon sufficient integrations by parts., and whose infrared scaling properties determine the fate of the theory. This structure bears a resemblance to the role played by vertex operators in the Coulomb-gas and sine-Gordon descriptions of the Berezinskii–Kosterlitz–Thouless (BKT) transition[5,6,29,30,31,28,1], where the infrared relevance of the vertex operators determines whether vortex fugacity grows or decreases under coarse graining.

Although this may seem as only a passing analogy given that we are working in an intrinsically2+12+1-dimensional setup, it is a noteworthy feature that our model exhibits a BKT-like renormalization group structure characterized by a marginal vertex instability. Unlike the conventional BKT transition, however, the instability emerges from an anisotropic soft mode rather than a logarithmic Coulomb-gas sector, and its interpretation in terms of vortex unbinding remains unclear. In both cases, the infrared behavior is controlled by a line of fixed points and a change in the relevance of a vertex nonlinearity. However, the analogy should be understood at the level of universality classes rather than microphysics – unlike the conventional BKT setup, isotropic Malthusian flocks are governed by an anisotropic soft mode, whose equal-time propagator takes the form (62). Consequently, the infrared fluctuations are not generated by logarithmically scaling Green’s functions that underlie the Coulomb-gas and sine-Gordon descriptions of the BKT transition. Rather, the transition emerges from the interplay between anisotropic soft fluctuations and symmetry-protected vertex nonlinearities in a genuinely2+12+1-dimensional stochastic field theory.

A further distinction from the conventional BKT scenario arises from the nonlinearly realized symmetry of the MSRDJ action (34), which combines shifts of the flock angle field with time-dependent spatial rotations. This symmetry forbids the generation of non-derivatively coupled terms capable of pinning the soft mode. Consequently, although the vertex operators become infrared relevant, and drive the theory into a symmetry-protected and potentially strongly coupled regime forΔ/κ<2​π\Delta/\kappa<2\pi, their interactions cannot generate a local potential forψ\psior destroy the gapless excitation. The strong-coupling phase therefore remains gapless despite the onset of nonperturbative infrared dynamics. Whether there exists a non-trivial Lifshitz scaling analog of the dual Coulomb-gas or sine-Gordon picture for the isotropic Malthusian flock presents itself as an important open question to us, and warrants further investigation.

## VIConcluding remarks and future directions

In our attempt to make sense of the nature of quasi-long-range order in active flocks, we inadvertently did what anyone who naturally thinks along the lines of a particle physicist would have done – proceeded to work in the simplest, most symmetric case to get our bearings, regardless of whether it represented a phenomenologically interesting corner of parameter space. By setting up the renormalization of the Malthusian flock within the MSRDJ formalism in the limit of isotropic diffusion, we chanced upon a theory with a remarkable set of invariances that facilitated its statistical field theoretic renormalization to all orders. In doing so, we set up a theoretical laboratory within which we could meaningfully compare and contrast various claims and counterclaims in the literature (with a technical summary provided in AppendixF), and arrived at an understanding as to how certain assumptions made in the literature may not always withstand the scrutiny of a complete diagrammatic or functional analysis.

We uncovered a rich phase structure, controlled by the ratio of the variance of the noiseΔ\Deltato the isotropic diffusion coefficientκ\kappa. The theory exhibits a continuous line of fixed points together with a marginal vertex instability at the critical valueΔ/κ=2​π\Delta/\kappa=2\pi. This instability marks the boundary between a Gaussian phase and a strongly interacting symmetry-protected gapless phase, thereby realizing a form of nonequilibrium criticality distinct from conventional Wilson–Fisher universality. Gapless excitations persist on both sides of the transition, implying the persistence of quasi-long-range order forΔ/κ<2​π\Delta/\kappa<2\pi.

We must of course, caution against extrapolating too far from the conclusions presented here. Foremost, the debate on the nature of long-range order in active flocks more broadly is far from settled by our work. It behooves us to study the perturbative renormalization of the anisotropic deformation of the Malthusian flock, in which numerous additional derivatively coupled operators feature, to say nothing of the extension to immortal flocks. Much work remains to be done if the framework presented in this paper is to extend to more interesting models of active flocks.

## Appendix ALoop Corrections in the MSRDJ Formalism

In the various subsections of this appendix, we detail the computation of loop corrections to the parameters and fields of Malthusian flocking theory within the MSRDJ formulation. We start by elaborating on the Feynman rules by first identifying the propagators and vertices in the background field method. We then perform a power counting analysis of the perturbative expansion, and immediately infer various non-renormalization theorems that follow directly from the kinematics of the theory alone. We then proceed to compute vertex and propagator corrections, and elaborate on the one-loop corrections to the latter, where one immediately encounters diagrams that diverge. Upon summation over all diagrams contributing to the renormalization of a particular process, we find cancellations that reduce the superficial degree of divergence down to logarithmic divergences for the couplings and anomalous dimensions of the theory. We then extend our analysis to all loop orders, facilitated by the non-linear realization of the symmetries and field redundancies discussed in SectionIVand their associated Ward identities. In the final section of this appendix, we re-frame our results within the framework of Wilsonian renormalization.

We begin by noting that the free Lagrangian (16a) can be expressed asℒfree=12​Ψ⊺​M​Ψ,{\cal L}_{\rm free}=\frac{1}{2}\Psi^{\intercal}M\Psi,(85)

where the field vectorΨ\Psiand operator matrixMMare given byΨ\displaystyle\Psi=(ψϕ),\displaystyle=\binom{\psi}{\phi},M\displaystyle M=(0𝒪𝒪~−2​Δ),\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}0&{\cal O}\\
\widetilde{{\cal O}}&-2\Delta\end{pmatrix},(86)

and where the differential operators𝒪{\cal O}and𝒪~\widetilde{{\cal O}}are defined as𝒪\displaystyle{\cal O}≡−(∂t+λ​∂x+κ​∇2),\displaystyle\equiv-(\partial_{t}+\lambda\partial_{x}+\kappa\nabla^{2}),𝒪~\displaystyle\widetilde{{\cal O}}≡∂t+λ​∂x−κ​∇2.\displaystyle\equiv\partial_{t}+\lambda\partial_{x}-\kappa\nabla^{2}.(87)

We Fourier transform the fields asΨ​(t,𝐱)=∫ω,𝐤e−i​(ω​t−𝐤⋅𝐱)​Ψω,𝐤,\Psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})=\int_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}e^{-i(\omega t-{\mathbf{k}}\cdot{\mathbf{x}})}\,\Psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}},(88)

where∫ω,𝐤:=∫𝑑¯​ω​𝑑¯d​k\int_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}:=\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega\,d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}kandd​¯​ω=d​ω2​πd\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\omega=\frac{d\omega}{2\pi}andd​¯d​k=dd​k(2​π)dd\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}k=\frac{d^{d}k}{(2\pi)^{d}}. The free action can thus be written as:Sfree=12​∫t,𝐱,ν,𝐪,ω,𝐤e−i​[(ω+ν)​t−(𝐤+𝐪)⋅𝐱]​Ψν,𝐪⊺​(0𝒪ω,𝐤𝒪~ω,𝐤2​Δ)​Ψω,𝐤,\displaystyle S_{\rm free}=\frac{1}{2}\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}},\nu,{\mathbf{q}},\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}e^{-i[(\omega+\nu)t-({\mathbf{k}}+{\mathbf{q}})\cdot{\mathbf{x}}]}\Psi_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}^{\intercal}\begin{pmatrix}0&{\cal O}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\\
\widetilde{{\cal O}}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}&2\Delta\end{pmatrix}\Psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}},(89)

where𝒪ω,𝐤\displaystyle{\cal O}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}=i​(ω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2),\displaystyle=i(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}),𝒪~ω,𝐤\displaystyle\widetilde{{\cal O}}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}=−i​(ω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2).\displaystyle=-i(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}).(90)

We note that𝒪−ω,−𝐤\displaystyle{\cal O}_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}=𝒪~ω,𝐤,\displaystyle=\widetilde{{\cal O}}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}},𝒪~−ω,−𝐤\displaystyle\widetilde{{\cal O}}_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}=𝒪ω,𝐤.\displaystyle={\cal O}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}.(91)

Implicit in the above and in what follows is the fact that we have invoked the background field method:Ψ​(x,t)=ψ0+ψ​(x,t),Φ​(x,t)=ϕ0+ϕ​(x,t),\Psi(\textbf{x},t)=\psi_{0}+\psi(\textbf{x},t),~~\Phi(\textbf{x},t)=\phi_{0}+\phi(\textbf{x},t),(92)

whereψ0\psi_{0}andϕ0\phi_{0}correspond to stable saddle points of the MSRDJ, which we take to beψ0=ϕ0≡0\psi_{0}=\phi_{0}\equiv 0. -\widetilde{{\cal O}}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}&0\end{pmatrix}=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{2\Delta}{(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2})}&\frac{i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}}\\

fromMM, its inverse. Thus, the free propagator, denoted with a 0 subscript, is given by the transpose:⟨Ψ−ω,−𝐤​Ψω,𝐤⊺⟩0\displaystyle\langle\Psi_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}^{\phantom{\intercal}}\Psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}^{\intercal}\rangle_{0}=𝒢−ω,−𝐤\displaystyle={\cal G}_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}=𝒢ω,𝐤⊺\displaystyle={\cal G}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}^{\intercal}=(2​Δ(ω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)​(ω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2)−iω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2iω−λ​kx+i​κ​k20).\displaystyle=\begin{pmatrix}\frac{2\Delta}{(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2})}&\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}\\
\frac{i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}}&0\end{pmatrix}.(98)

Diagrammatrically, we represent the individual propagators as≡⟨ψ−ω,𝐤​ψω,𝐤⟩0=2​Δ(ω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)​(ω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2),\displaystyle\equiv\langle\psi_{-\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\,\psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}=\frac{2\Delta}{(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2})},(99a)≡⟨ϕ−ω,−𝐤​ψω,𝐤⟩0=iω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2,\displaystyle\equiv\langle\phi_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}\,\psi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}=\frac{i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}},(99b)≡⟨ψ−ω,−𝐤​ϕω,𝐤⟩0=−iω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2.\displaystyle\equiv\langle\psi_{-\omega,-{\mathbf{k}}}\,\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}=\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}.(99c)

In particular, we note the usual absence of a freeϕ\phi-ϕ\phiresponse field propagator[21,44].

From (16b), one can read off the(2​n+1)(2n+1)- and(2​n+2)(2n+2)-point interaction Lagrangians, wheren=1,2,3,…n=1,2,3,\ldots, which read:ℒint(2​n+1)\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}^{(2n+1)}=−(−1)n(2​n−1)!​λ​ϕ​ψ2​n−1​∂yψ,\displaystyle=-\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n-1)!}\lambda\phi\psi^{2n-1}\partial_{y}\psi,ℒint(2​n+2)\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}^{(2n+2)}=(−1)n(2​n)!​λ​ϕ​ψ2​n​∂xψ.\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}\lambda\phi\psi^{2n}\partial_{x}\psi.(100)

As usual, the factorials drop out of the final vertex factor due to the combinatorics of contracting external and internal fields, and with propagators attached at the vertex being amputated along with an overall momentum-conserving delta function. Thus, the vertex factors areω,𝐤\omega,{\mathbf{k}}2​n\scriptstyle 2n=(−1)n​i​λ​ky,\displaystyle=(-1)^{n}i\lambda k_{y},ω,𝐤\omega,{\mathbf{k}}2​n+1\scriptstyle 2n+1=−(−1)n​i​λ​kx,\displaystyle=-(-1)^{n}i\lambda k_{x},(101)

where in the above, we have takenω,𝐤\omega,{\mathbf{k}}to be the incoming frequency and momentum of the externalψ\psifield that is contracted with the internalϕ\phifield.

We note that despite the fact that the derivative does not act on theϕ\phifield in the interaction Lagrangian (16b), the vertex factor does eventually depend only on the momentum entering the vertex via the internalϕ\phifield upon enforcing momentum conservation at the vertex, which is equivalent to integrating by parts at the level of the action131313All variations are consistent with the boundary conditionsψ,ϕ→0\psi,\phi\to 0as|x|→∞|\textbf{x}|\to\infty. Note that the symmetries and field redundancies discussed in Sec.IVadmit a one-parameter asymptotic symmetry that relates all boundary configurations of the formϕ=0,ψ=c\phi=0,\psi=cfor some constantcc, the significance of which will be followed up on in future work.. That is, an equivalent way of proceeding would have been by integrating the interaction Lagrangian by parts from the outset, so thatℒint≡−λ​(sin⁡ψ−ψ)​∂xϕ−λ​(cos⁡ψ)​∂yϕ.\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}\equiv-\lambda(\sin\psi-\psi)\partial_{x}\phi-\lambda(\cos\psi)\partial_{y}\phi.(102)

Adding a factor ofλ​∂yϕ\lambda\partial_{y}\phito the above leaves the action invariant (given that it is a total derivative), so that equivalently, the interaction Lagrangian can be expressed as:ℒint=−λ​(sin⁡ψ−ψ)​∂xϕ−λ​(cos⁡ψ−1)​∂yϕ,\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}=-\lambda(\sin\psi-\psi)\partial_{x}\phi-\lambda(\cos\psi-1)\partial_{y}\phi,(103)

corresponding to the interaction termsℒint(2​n+1)\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}^{(2n+1)}=(−1)n(2​n)!​λ​ψ2​n​∂yϕ,\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}\lambda\psi^{2n}\partial_{y}\phi,ℒint(2​n+2)\displaystyle{\cal L}_{\rm int}^{(2n+2)}=−(−1)n(2​n+1)!​λ​ψ2​n+1​∂xϕ,\displaystyle=-\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)!}\lambda\psi^{2n+1}\partial_{x}\phi,(104)

which reproduce the same vertex factors as (101).

## A.1Non-Renormalization Theorems and Power Counting

There are a set of non-renormalization theorems that one can immediately infer directly from the kinematic structure of the propagators. The proof proceeds exactly as presented in[8]141414We elaborate on additional relations between anomalous dimensions, along with the form invariance of the advection potential under renormalization group improvement enforced by various Ward identities in Sec.IV..
