}}(105)

Each internal propagator has the form𝒢ϕ​ψ\displaystyle\mathcal{G}_{\phi\psi}=−iω−Ω​(𝐤),\displaystyle=\frac{-i}{\omega-\Omega({\mathbf{k}})},Ω​(𝐤)\displaystyle\Omega({\mathbf{k}})=λ​kx+i​κ​k2.\displaystyle=\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}.(106)

The important thing to note is that the imaginary part ofΩ​(𝐤)\Omega({\mathbf{k}})is always positive (assumingκ\kappais positive). Then,ΓN\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}=∫𝑑¯​ν​𝑑¯d​q​−i​V1​(k1,−q−k1)ν+ω1−Ω​(𝐪+𝐤1)⋅−i​V2​(k2,−q−k1−k2)ν+ω1+ω2−Ω​(𝐪+𝐤1+𝐤2)​⋯​−i​VN​(kN,−q)ν−Ω​(𝐪).\displaystyle=\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\,\frac{-iV_{1}(k_{1},-q-k_{1})}{\nu+\omega_{1}-\Omega({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{k}}_{1})}\cdot\frac{-iV_{2}(k_{2},-q-k_{1}-k_{2})}{\nu+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\Omega({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2})}\cdots\frac{-iV_{N}(k_{N},-q)}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})}.(107)

Of course, for the theory at hand, the vertex only actually depends on the incoming momentum of the dashed line at the vertex:VN​(kN,−q)=VN​(−q)V_{N}(k_{N},-q)=V_{N}(-q), etc.

Note that all of the poles of the integrand are located in the upper half-plane inν\nu. If there are no factors of frequency appearing in the vertices (i.e., the interaction vertices do not involve time derivatives), and ifN≥2N\geq 2, then we can always complete the integration contour in the lower half-plane, thereby proving thatΓN=0\Gamma_{N}=0. ForN≥2N\geq 2, the assumption about there being no factors of the frequency in the vertices may be relaxed to the assumption that there be at mostN−2N-2factors of frequency appearing in the vertices in total, for, in that case, the integrand still decays at least as fast as|ν|−2|\nu|^{-2}at large|ν||\nu|and we may still close the contour in the lower half-plane, thereby proving thatΓN=0\Gamma_{N}=0. This relaxed assumption is not necessary anyway because there are no interactions involving time derivatives in this theory. Note thatN=1N=1is not technically captured under this argument because the integrand goes likeν−1\nu^{-1}in that case and that is not fast enough to justify the usual procedure of closing the contour (the contribution of the infinite semi-circle at infinity does not necessarily vanish), and will be dealt with as a separate case shortly.

We also note that a diagram that containsΓ\Gammaas a subdiagram will automatically be accompanied by the same diagram withΓ\Gammareplaced with the subdiagramΓ′\Gamma^{\prime}, which is given by changing all of the internal propagators to be𝒢ψ​ϕ\mathcal{G}_{\psi\phi}rather than𝒢ϕ​ψ\mathcal{G}_{\phi\psi}:ΓN′≡xVN′xV1′xV2′xVn′kNk1k2knqq+k1q+k1+⋯+kn−1q+k1+⋯+kn\Gamma_{N}^{\prime}\equiv\vbox{\hbox{

}}(108)

This is given byΓN′\displaystyle\Gamma_{N}^{\prime}=∫𝑑¯​ν​𝑑¯d​q​i​V1′​(q,k1)ν+ω1−Ω​(𝐪+𝐤1)∗⋅i​V2′​(q+k1,k2)ν+ω1+ω2−Ω​(𝐪+𝐤1+𝐤2)∗​⋯​i​VN′​(q−kN,kN)ν−Ω​(𝐪)∗,\displaystyle=\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\,\frac{iV_{1}^{\prime}(q,k_{1})}{\nu+\omega_{1}-\Omega({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{k}}_{1})^{*}}\cdot\frac{iV_{2}^{\prime}(q+k_{1},k_{2})}{\nu+\omega_{1}+\omega_{2}-\Omega({\mathbf{q}}+{\mathbf{k}}_{1}+{\mathbf{k}}_{2})^{*}}\cdots\frac{iV_{N}^{\prime}(q-k_{N},k_{N})}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})^{*}},(109)

where we have used the fact that𝒢ψ​ϕ=𝒢ϕ​ψ∗\mathcal{G}_{\psi\phi}=\mathcal{G}_{\phi\psi}^{*}. Now, all of the poles are in the lower half-plane. Again, ifN≥2N\geq 2and there are fewer thanN−2N-2factors of frequency appearing in the vertices in total, then, we can always close the contour in the upper half-plane, thereby proving thatΓN′=0\Gamma_{N}^{\prime}=0.

AlthoughΓ1\Gamma_{1}andΓ1′\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}do not necessarily vanish separately, it turns out that their sum, given byΓ1+Γ1′=∫𝑑¯​ν​𝑑¯d​q​(−i​V​(−q)ν−Ω​(𝐪)+i​V​(q)ν−Ω​(𝐪)∗).\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\biggl(\frac{-iV(-q)}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})}+\frac{iV(q)}{\nu-\Omega({\mathbf{q}})^{*}}\biggr).(110)

does indeed vanish. This follows from the fact that the vertices (101) are linear in momentum and purely imaginary,V​(−q)=−V​(q)V(-q)=-V(q)and[−i​V​(−q)]∗=i​V​(q)[-iV(-q)]^{*}=iV(q), so thatΓ1′=Γ1∗\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}^{*}, andΓ1+Γ1′=2​i​∫𝑑¯d​q​V​(q)​∫𝑑¯​ν​ν−Re​Ω​(𝐪)(ν−Re​Ω​(𝐪))2+(Im​Ω​(𝐪))2.\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=2i\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{d}q\,V(q)\int d\hskip-0.80002pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\nu\,\frac{\nu-\text{Re}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}})}{(\nu-\text{Re}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}}))^{2}+(\text{Im}\Omega({\mathbf{q}}))^{2}}.(111)

One can change the integration variable to the shifted frequencyν~=ν−Re​Ω​(𝐪)\tilde{\nu}=\nu-\text{Re}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}}). ProvidedIm​Ω​(𝐪)≠0\text{Im}\,\Omega({\mathbf{q}})\neq 0, the numerator is odd inν~\tilde{\nu}while the denominator is even, and so the integral vanishes, so thatΓ1+Γ1′=Γ1+Γ1∗=0.\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{\prime}=\Gamma_{1}+\Gamma_{1}^{*}=0.(112)

Therefore, the nonrenormalization theorem applies to the caseN=1N=1as well asN≥2N\geq 2.

Let us momentarily relax the isotropic assumption so thatκ\kappasplits intoκx\kappa_{x}andκy\kappa_{y}in each direction, respectively151515We note that this is not to be understood in terms of the more general model for Malthusian flocks referenced in[9,13,10,12,11], where additional derivatively-coupled operators appear with coefficients that vanish in the limitκx→κy\kappa_{x}\to\kappa_{y}. Rather, we consider the deformation of the isotropic diffusion term alone so that it can have differingκx\kappa_{x}andκy\kappa_{y}.. Assign the followingbaredimensions:[t]\displaystyle[t]=−z,\displaystyle=-z,[x]\displaystyle[x]=−ζ,\displaystyle=-\zeta,[y]\displaystyle[y]=−1,\displaystyle=-1,[ℒ]\displaystyle[{\cal L}]=1+ζ+z.\displaystyle=1+\zeta+z.(113)

The form of the free Lagrangian (16a) then implies that:1+ζ+z\displaystyle 1+\zeta+z=[ϕ]+z+[ψ]=[ϕ]+[κx]+2​ζ+[ψ]=[ϕ]+[κy]+2+[ψ]=[λ]+[ψ]+[ϕ]+ζ\displaystyle=[\phi]+z+[\psi]=[\phi]+[\kappa_{x}]+2\zeta+[\psi]=[\phi]+[\kappa_{y}]+2+[\psi]=[\lambda]+[\psi]+[\phi]+\zeta=[Δ]+2​[ϕ]=[λ]+[ϕ]+1,\displaystyle=[\Delta]+2[\phi]=[\lambda]+[\phi]+1,(114)

which are equivalent to the relations[ϕ]+[ψ]\displaystyle[\phi]+[\psi]=1+ζ,[κx]=z−2​ζ,\displaystyle=1+\zeta,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa_{x}]=z-2\zeta,(115a)[λ]\displaystyle[\lambda]=z−ζ,[κy]=z−2,\displaystyle=z-\zeta,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa_{y}]=z-2,(115b)[Δ]+2​[ϕ]\displaystyle[\Delta]+2[\phi]=1+ζ+z,[λ]+[ϕ]=ζ+z.\displaystyle=1+\zeta+z,\hskip 32.0pt[\lambda]+[\phi]=\zeta+z.(115c)

Of course,ψ\psimust be dimensionless in order to retain its interpretation as an angle, for otherwise, we would not be able to take its cosine or sine. This is of course, a direct corollary of the power-counting renormalizability of allz=2z=2actions in 2+1 dimensions with up to four spatial derivatives[23,50,16,17]. Therefore,[ψ]=0,[\psi]=0,(116)

from which it follows that[ϕ]\displaystyle[\phi]=1+ζ,[κx]=z−2​ζ,\displaystyle=1+\zeta,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa_{x}]=z-2\zeta,(117a)[κy]\displaystyle[\kappa_{y}]=z−2,[λ]=z−ζ,\displaystyle=z-2,\hskip 50.0pt[\lambda]=z-\zeta,(117b)[Δ]\displaystyle[\Delta]=z−ζ−1,[λ]=z−1.\displaystyle=z-\zeta-1,\hskip 31.0pt[\lambda]=z-1.(117c)

The last two equations of (117b) and (117c) then imply thatζ=1,\zeta=1,(118)

and so the dimensions[x][x]and[y][y]are identical, and[ϕ]\displaystyle[\phi]=2,[κ]=z−2,\displaystyle=2,\hskip 50.0pt[\kappa]=z-2,(119a)[λ]\displaystyle[\lambda]=z−1,[Δ]=z−2,\displaystyle=z-1,\hskip 32.0pt[\Delta]=z-2,(119b)

where[κx]=[κy]=[κ][\kappa_{x}]=[\kappa_{y}]=[\kappa]follows by corollary. At this point, it is argued within the context of a dynamical renormalization group analysis in[13]that in order to keepκx\kappa_{x},κy\kappa_{y}andΔ\Deltafixed, or, in other words, to make them dimensionless, we have to pickz=2.z=2.(120)

It follows immediately that[λ]=1[\lambda]=1and soλ\lambdais arelevantparameter at bare level.

## A.2Organization of the Diagrammatic Expansion

Define the following quantities:L\displaystyle L=#​of loops,\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of loops},(121a)I\displaystyle I=#​of internal lines,\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of internal lines},(121b)E\displaystyle E=#​of external lines,\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of external lines},(121c)V2​n+1\displaystyle V_{2n+1}=#​of(2​n+1)-vertices,\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of $(2n+1)$-vertices},(121d)V2​n+2\displaystyle V_{2n+2}=#​of(2​n+2)-vertices.\displaystyle=\#\ \text{of $(2n+2)$-vertices}.(121e)

There are two simple relations one can immediately derive:L\displaystyle L=I−∑n=1∞(V2​n+1+V2​n+2)+1,\displaystyle=I-\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(V_{2n+1}+V_{2n+2})+1,(122a)2​I+E\displaystyle 2I+E=∑n=1∞[(2​n+1)​V2​n+1+(2​n+2)​V2​n+2].\displaystyle=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\bigl[(2n+1)V_{2n+1}+(2n+2)V_{2n+2}\bigr].(122b)

The first equation comes from the fact that every internal line gets its own momentum to be integrated over, but each vertex imposes one momentum conservation relation among those momenta, except that those relations are themselves not all independent as there is one relation among them for overall momentum conservation.

The second equation simply counts the total number of endpoints of lines that must be attached on all of the vertices. Every vertex of typeV2​n+1V_{2n+1}has2​n+12n+1lines and every vertex of typeV2​n+2V_{2n+2}has2​n+22n+2lines. These must be exhausted by all of the internal and external lines, except that internal lines contribute both of their endpoints while external lines only contribute one.

## A.2.1Corrections to the Propagators

For propagators,E=2E=2and we can writeI=−1+∑n=1∞((2​n+1)​V2​n+12+(n+1)​V2​n+2),I=-1+\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2n+1)\frac{V_{2n+1}}{2}+(n+1)V_{2n+2}\biggr),(123)

and thus,L=∑n=1∞((2​n−1)​V2​n+12+n​V2​n+2).L=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2n-1)\frac{V_{2n+1}}{2}+nV_{2n+2}\biggr).(124)

Clearly, the sum of all theV2​n+1V_{2n+1}coefficients must be even. This is because each2​n+12n+1vertex comes with an odd number of fields, and given that they must be pairwise contracted in order to form a diagram, there is no way this can be done if the total number of fields is odd. Therefore we need the total number of such2​n+12n+1vertices, i.e. the sum of all theV2​n+1V_{2n+1}to be an even number.

## A.2.2Corrections to the Vertices

The corrections to the(2​n+2)(2n+2)-vertex will haveE=2​n+2E=2n+2, so thatI=−(n+1)+∑m=1∞((2​m+1)​V2​m+12+(m+1)​V2​m+2),I=-(n+1)+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m+1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+(m+1)V_{2m+2}\biggr),(125)

andL=−n+∑m=1∞((2​m−1)​V2​m+12+m​V2​m+2).L=-n+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m-1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+mV_{2m+2}\biggr).(126)

Note that (123) and (124) are simply then=0n=0versions of (125) and (126), respectively. As with the propagator corrections, the sum of theV2​m+1V_{2m+1}coefficients must be even.

On the other hand, the corrections toV2​n+1V_{2n+1}haveE=2​n+1E=2n+1, and soI=−(n+12)+∑m=1∞((2​m+1)​V2​m+12+(m+1)​V2​m+2),I=-\biggl(n+\frac{1}{2}\biggr)+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m+1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+(m+1)V_{2m+2}\biggr),(127)

andL=−n+12+∑m=1∞((2​m−1)​V2​m+12+m​V2​m+2).L=-n+\frac{1}{2}+\sum_{m=1}^{\infty}\biggl((2m-1)\frac{V_{2m+1}}{2}+mV_{2m+2}\biggr).(128)

In this case, the sum of theV2​m+1V_{2m+1}’s must be odd.

For the one-loop corrections to the propagators,L=1L=1in (124) implies thatV≥5=0V_{\geq 5}=0, and so1=V32+V4.1=\frac{V_{3}}{2}+V_{4}.(129)

The possible values ofV3V_{3}andV4V_{4}are tabulated in Table1along with the structure of the corresponding diagram. ## A.4Superficial Degree of Divergence

The power-counting classification presented in sec.A.1has important consequences for the superficial degree of divergence of the various Feynman diagrams. A diagram with aψ\psi-ψ\psipropagator in an internal line will always have a lower degree of divergence compared to a diagram with the same structure, but where theψ\psi-ψ\psipropagator is replaced with either aϕ\phi-ψ\psior aψ\psi-ϕ\phipropagator. In fact, the degree of divergence is reduced by 2 for each internalϕ\phi-ψ\psiorψ\psi-ϕ\phipropagator replaced with aψ\psi-ψ\psipropagator, and further reduced by 1 because one vertex factor will now contain an external rather than an internal momentum factor.

Let us compute the superficial degree of divergence for arbitraryzzand spatial dimensionddbefore plugging inz=d=2z=d=2. A diagram withLLloops will haveLLinternal frequency-momentum integrals for a total dimension of(z+d)​L(z+d)L. Each internal line comes with a factor of a propagator. Let us splitIIup into the number ofϕ\phi-ψ\psiorψ\psi-ϕ\phiinternal lines and the number ofψ\psi-ψ\psiinternal lines:I=Iϕ​ψ+Iψ​ψ.I=I_{\phi\psi}+I_{\psi\psi}.(134)

Each internal line comes with a corresponding propagator factor, which has dimension−z-zfor aϕ\phi-ψ\psiorψ\psi-ϕ\philine and dimension−2​z-2zfor aψ\psi-ψ\psiline. Furthermore, eachIϕ​ψI_{\phi\psi}contributes one factor of internal momentum due to the vertex. Therefore, the superficial degree of divergence,DD, of any diagram isD\displaystyle D=(z+d)​L−z​(Iϕ​ψ+2​Iψ​ψ)+Iϕ​ψ\displaystyle=(z+d)L-z(I_{\phi\psi}+2I_{\psi\psi})+I_{\phi\psi}=(z+d)​L−2​z​Iψ​ψ−(z−1)​Iϕ​ψ\displaystyle=(z+d)L-2zI_{\psi\psi}-(z-1)I_{\phi\psi}=(z+d)​L−2​z​I+(z+1)​Iϕ​ψ.\displaystyle=(z+d)L-2zI+(z+1)I_{\phi\psi}.(135)

Plugging inz=d=2z=d=2givesD=4​(L−I)+3​Iϕ​ψ.D=4(L-I)+3I_{\phi\psi}.(136)

Indeed, if we reduce the number of internalϕ\phi-ψ\psilines by one, so thatIϕ​ψ→Iϕ​ψ−1I_{\phi\psi}\to I_{\phi\psi}-1, then the superficial degree of divergence of the diagram is reduced by 3, as previously described.

## Appendix BOne-Loop Order – Regularization and Scheme Independence of Log Divergences

The simple dimension counting of the previous subsections have very important consequences. For example, for one-loop diagrams, one hasD=4−4​I+3​Iϕ​ψ.D=4-4I+3I_{\phi\psi}.(137)

The maximum value forIϕ​ψI_{\phi\psi}isII, and soDmax=4−I.D_{\rm max}=4-I.(138)

We considerI≥2I\geq 2andI=1I=1as separate cases. ForI≥2I\geq 2, one hasDmax≤2D_{\rm max}\leq 2, and so we need only consider the caseIϕ​ψ=II_{\phi\psi}=I, as reducingIϕ​ψI_{\phi\psi}would reduceDDby at least 3, thereby givingD≤−1D\leq-1which correspond to UV-finite diagrams that do not require renormalization. The caseI=1I=1impliesDmax=3D_{\rm max}=3, where we could either haveIϕ​ψ=1I_{\phi\psi}=1for whichD=3D=3, orIϕ​ψ=0I_{\phi\psi}=0, for whichD=0D=0(i.e., corresponding to a log-divergence). However, ifIϕ​ψ=II_{\phi\psi}=I, the process involves the sum of loop diagrams whereϕ\phi-ψ\psiandψ\psi-ϕ\philines are interchanged, and therefore vanishes according to the non-renormalization theorem in sec.A.1. Hence, only one family of diagrams contributes to the renormalization of the parameters of our theory at one loop:(I,Iϕ​ψ)=(1,0)(I,I_{\phi\psi})=(1,0), which correspond to log-divergences.

For theϕ\phi-ψ\psipropagator, the relevant one loop diagram is thus given by:=Mϕ​ψ(1).\vbox{\hbox{

}}=M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}.(139)

Here, we are using the standard convention wherein the external propagators of the diagram are amputated161616Although this convention arises from the necessities of LSZ reduction in quantum field theory[24,40,7], it persists in statistical field theory in isolating the interacting parts of the one particle irreducible diagrams given the role of external propagators in simply tracking momentum in and outflow in a perturbative computation[21,44].. In so doing, this diagram is, in fact, the one loop correction to the inverse propagator, not the propagator itself. This is why we have denoted it byMM, not𝒢{\cal G}. This evaluates to−⟨ψ−ω,𝐤​ϕω,𝐤​13!​λ​∫ν,𝐪ϕν,𝐪​(i​qx)​∫1,2,3ψν1,𝐪1​ψν2,𝐪2​ψν3,𝐪3​δ​¯​(ν+∑j=13νj)​δ​¯(d)​(𝐪+∑j=13𝐪j)⟩0\displaystyle-\Biggl\langle\psi_{-\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\frac{1}{3!}\lambda\int_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}\phi_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}(iq_{x})\int_{1,2,3}\psi_{\nu_{1},{\mathbf{q}}_{1}}\psi_{\nu_{2},{\mathbf{q}}_{2}}\psi_{\nu_{3},{\mathbf{q}}_{3}}\,\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt\biggl(\nu+\sum_{j=1}^{3}\nu_{j}\biggr)\delta\hskip-2.40005pt\bar{}\hskip 1.00006pt^{(d)}\biggl({\mathbf{q}}+\sum_{j=1}^{3}{\mathbf{q}}_{j}\biggr)\Biggr\rangle_{0}=−i​λ2​(−iω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)​kx​(−iω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)​∫ν,𝐪2​Δ(ν−λ​qx−i​κ​q2)​(ν−λ​qx+i​κ​q2)\displaystyle=-\frac{i\lambda}{2}\biggl(\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}\biggr)k_{x}\biggl(\frac{-i}{\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}\biggr)\int_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{2\Delta}{(\nu-\lambda q_{x}-i\kappa q^{2})(\nu-\lambda q_{x}+i\kappa q^{2})}=−i​λ​kx​ℐ​⟨ψ−ω,𝐤​ϕω,𝐤⟩02,\displaystyle=-i\lambda k_{x}\mathscr{I}\langle\psi_{-\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\phi_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}\rangle_{0}^{2},(140)

whereℐ=Δ​∫ν,𝐪1(ν−λ​qx−i​κ​q2)​(ν−λ​qx+i​κ​q2)=Δ​∫ν~,𝐪1ν~2+(κ​q2)2,\mathscr{I}=\Delta\int_{\nu,{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{1}{(\nu-\lambda q_{x}-i\kappa q^{2})(\nu-\lambda q_{x}+i\kappa q^{2})}=\Delta\int_{\widetilde{\nu},{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{1}{\widetilde{\nu}^{2}+(\kappa q^{2})^{2}},(141)

andν~=ν−λ​qx.\widetilde{\nu}=\nu-\lambda q_{x}.(142)

Theν~\widetilde{\nu}integral is straightforward to compute either using the calculus of residues or a trigonometric substitution, and the result isℐ=Δ2​κ​∫𝐪1q2→d=2Δ4​π​κ​∫0∞d​qq→regΔ4​π​κ​ln⁡Λμ,\mathscr{I}=\frac{\Delta}{2\kappa}\int_{{\mathbf{q}}}\frac{1}{q^{2}}\xrightarrow{d=2}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q}\xrightarrow{\rm reg}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\ln\frac{\Lambda}{\mu},(143)

where “reg” stands for “regularized,” implemented most directly with hard UV and IR cut-offs,Λ\Lambdaandμ\mu, respectively. Ideally, we should ensure that this would be the result in any scheme, given that the coefficients of the leading short distance logarithmic divergences dictate the corresponding coefficients of the beta functions, and therefore the renormalization group flow of the associated couplings.

Fortunately, the coefficients of leading short-distance logarithmic divergences are scheme-independent. That is, for any regularization scheme, whether hard cutoff, zeta-function, Pauli-Villars, point-split, dimensional regularization, etc., the leading log divergences can explicitly be shown to have the same coefficient (see e.g.[7,37]). This is an important enough (and often misunderstood) point, that is worth elaborating upon it in the present context.

One can, for example, arrive at the same coefficient for the leading logarithmic divergence in the context of dimensional regularization by first recasting the integral in (143) in terms of a related integral overℝ2\mathbb{R}^{2}:∫0∞d​qq=∫0∞q​d​qq2=12​π​∫−∞∞d2​qq2,\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q}=\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{q\,dq}{q^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}},(144)

and realizing that for integrals that include an explicit mass scalemm, that:ID​(m2)=∫dD​k(2​π)D​k2​A(k2+m2)B=Γ​(A+D2)​Γ​(B−A−D2)(4​π)D/2​Γ​(D2)​Γ​(B)​(m2)A−B+D/2.I_{D}(m^{2})=\int\frac{d^{D}k}{(2\pi)^{D}}\frac{k^{2A}}{(k^{2}+m^{2})^{B}}=\frac{\Gamma\left(A+\frac{D}{2}\right)\Gamma\left(B-A-\frac{D}{2}\right)}{(4\pi)^{D/2}\Gamma\left(\frac{D}{2}\right)\Gamma(B)}\left(m^{2}\right)^{A-B+D/2}.(145)

Although the integrand (144) is scaleless and therefore might appear to nominally vanish when dimensionally regularized, this vanishing can be traced to a cancellation of UV and IR contributions enforced by default in dimensional regularization. In order to see this, we expand the divergent integrand via partial fractions into the sum12​π​∫−∞∞d2​qq2=12​π​∫−∞∞d2​q​[1q2+m2+m2q2​(q2+m2)],\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}}=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}d^{2}q\left[\frac{1}{q^{2}+m^{2}}+\frac{m^{2}}{q^{2}(q^{2}+m^{2})}\right],(146)

wherem2m^{2}arises as an auxiliary mass scale via the partial fraction expansion that subsequently cancels between the two contributions. Of particular note is the fact that the first term on the right hand side of the above is UV-divergent, whereas the second term is IR-divergent. Evaluating both terms using (145) and expanding aroundD=2−δD=2-\deltadimensions results in equal and opposite contributions of the form±[1δ−12​(log⁡m24​π​μ2+γE)],\pm\left[\frac{1}{\delta}-\frac{1}{2}\left(\log\frac{m^{2}}{4\pi\mu^{2}}+\gamma_{E}\right)\right],(147)

whereμ\muis some arbitrary mass scale necessitated from dimensional deformation. Were we to label the UV and IR pole contributions separately, one arrives at the resultΔ4​π​κ​∫0∞d​qq→regΔ4​π​κ​[1δUV−1δIR+log⁡μUVμIR],\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\int_{0}^{\infty}\frac{dq}{q}\xrightarrow{\rm reg}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\left[\frac{1}{\delta_{\rm UV}}-\frac{1}{\delta_{\rm IR}}+\log\frac{\mu_{\rm UV}}{\mu_{\rm IR}}\right],(148)

which matches with (143) with the identificationsμUV≡Λ\mu_{\rm UV}\equiv\LambdaandμIR≡μ\mu_{\rm IR}\equiv\mu. Whatever scheme one works in, provided one is expanding around a background field that is astable saddle pointof the MSRDJ action, all IR divergences will cancel in well defined observables (and can be taken as the defining criteria for the latter)[7,37]. UV divergences get subtracted by appropriate counterterms, which, remarkably, can be absorbed via the renormalization of the couplings in the action (16a) and (16b), i.e., the theory is itselfrenormalizablein the particle physics sense, and does not need to be treated as an effective theory[7]. Given that it is the coefficient of the leading logarithmic short distance divergences that determine how couplings run with scale, we see that one would have arrived at the same running having implemented dimensional regularization properly, as one would have by naïvely imposing hard cut-offs.

An alternate manner in which one could have arrived at this same conclusion would have been through reexamining the statement that (143) legitimately corresponds to a scaleless integral. We note that implicit in the background field expansion invoked in our calculation is the fact thatψ0\psi_{0}andϕ0\phi_{0}in (92) correspond to stable saddle points of the MSRDJ action. Although taking both to be (vanishing) constants certainly meet this criteria, one will in reality have very long wavelength fluctuations of the background fields such that the net effect of expanding around them would be to generate an IR mass scale for the propagator in (144) arising from derivatives acting on the background fields171717Note that when a similar integral is encountered, for example, in[40]in the context of the non-linear sigma model in 1+1 dimensions, an IR regulator is introduced by hand. Strictly speaking, this should be understood in terms of the argument just presented, with the net result that the one loop beta functions are proportional to the target space curvature felt by slow variations of the background field., which we denote asμIR≡μBG\mu_{\rm IR}\equiv\mu_{\rm BG}so that one instead has to evaluate:I=12​π​∫−∞∞d2​qq2→12​π​∫−∞∞d2​qq2+μBG2,I=\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}}\rightarrow\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}+\mu_{\rm BG}^{2}},(149)

which now only manifests a UV divergence. Regularizing the above using (145) results inΔ4​π​κ​12​π​∫−∞∞d2​qq2+μBG2→regΔ4​π​κ​[1δUV+log⁡(μUVμBG)],\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\frac{1}{2\pi}\int_{-\infty}^{\infty}\frac{d^{2}q}{q^{2}+\mu_{\rm BG}^{2}}\xrightarrow{\rm reg}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\left[\frac{1}{\delta_{\rm UV}}+\log\left(\frac{\mu_{\rm UV}}{\mu_{\rm BG}}\right)\right],(150)

and will result in the same running as would have been extracted by hard cut-offs, or factorizing and dimensionally regularizing the nominally scaleless integral (143).

Thus satisfied by the scheme independence of the coefficient of the leading UV logarithmic divergence, we find that upon amputation of the externalϕ\phi-ψ\psipropagators, the inverse propagator gets the following scale dependent one loop correction:Mϕ​ψ(1)=−i​λ​kx​ℐ=−i​λ​kx​Δ4​π​κ​ln⁡Λμ.M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}=-i\lambda k_{x}\mathscr{I}=-i\lambda k_{x}\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}\ln\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}.(151)

Given that the bare value isMϕ​ψ(0)=O~ω,𝐤=−i​(ω−λ​kx+i​κ​k2)M_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}=\widetilde{O}_{\omega,{\mathbf{k}}}=-i(\omega-\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}), adding these contributions together results inMϕ​ψ(0)+Mϕ​ψ(1)=−i​[ω−(1−ℐ)​λ​kx+i​κ​k2].M_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}=-i\bigl[\omega-(1-\mathscr{I})\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}\bigr].(152)

At this point, one might be tempted to conclude that the only quantity that gets renormalized isλ\lambda. As we elaborate upon later, this is incorrect. Instead,λ\lambda,Δ\Delta,κ\kappa,ϕ\phi, and the spatial coordinates𝐱{\mathbf{x}}all receive anomalous dimensions, as given by (182) and elaborated upon in AppendixD.

We furthermore note that the correction to theϕ\phi-ψ\psiinverse propagator is the same as for theψ\psi-ϕ\phicase. In addition, no diagram of this type, that is with(I,Iϕ​ψ)=(1,0)(I,I_{\phi\psi})=(1,0), exists with both external fields beingϕ\phifields. Therefore, there is no correction toMϕ​ϕM_{\phi\phi}, or in other words,Mϕ​ϕ(1)=0M_{\phi\phi}^{(1)}=0andMϕ​ϕ(0)+Mϕ​ϕ(1)=Mϕ​ϕ(0)=2​Δ,M_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\phi}^{(1)}=M_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}=2\Delta,(153)

Once more, it might be tempting to conclude thatΔ\Deltadoes not get renormalized because there are no diagrammatic contributions to the above term. This is in fact precisely the argument made by Chaté and Solon in[9]. However, this argument is not entirely correct. As we discuss in detail in AppendixD,Δ\Deltadoes indeed get an anomalous dimension as per (182), and this can be traced to the peculiarities of any Lifshitz theory withz≠1z\neq 1.

We are now in a position to complete the calculation of the one-loop correction to the propagators. There are two diagrams of the following type that contribute toMψ​ψM_{\psi\psi}:

}}=M_{\psi\psi}^{(1)}.(154)

However, these two diagrams are exactly the same except that their vertex factors have the opposite sign to each other. Therefore, they cancel and, sinceMψ​ψ(0)=0M_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}=0, we find that simply:Mψ​ψ(0)+Mψ​ψ(1)=0.M_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\psi\psi}^{(1)}=0.(155)

Shifting our attention now to the one-loop correction to the vertices: Each vertex contains the same coupling constant,λ\lambda, and this factor is the same as the one appearing in the propagator. A priori, there is no reason why these should get renormalized uniformly, nor in the same manner as within the propagator. That is, the advection termsλ​𝐧​(ψ)⋅∇ψ\lambda{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla\psiandλ​𝐧​(ψ)⋅∇ϕ\lambda{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla\phicould in principle get deformed into an arbitrary derivative expansion that need not resum into an analytic form, let alone the special trigonometric forms appearing in our theory. That it does to all loop orders is a very special property of our model, which is ultimately due to the nonlinear realization of the symmetries and field redundancies identified in SectionIV. In operational terms, we can see this via the following argument: The only one-loop diagram that contributes to the renormalization of the(2​n+1)(2n+1)-vertexV2​n+1V_{2n+1}is theV2​n+3V_{2n+3}vertex with two of the internalψ\psilines contracted to form a loop. The externalϕ\phifield and2​n2nout of the2​n+12n+1externalψ\psifields must be contracted with2​n+12n+1of the2​n+32n+3internalψ\psifields. That gives a combinatoric factor of12​[(2​n+3)!]\frac{1}{2}[(2n+3)!]. The factor of12\frac{1}{2}is the symmetry factor of the diagram. The result of the computation of the loop integral proceeds as before and gives the resultℐ\mathscr{I}. Since there is a relative minus sign betweenV2​n+3V_{2n+3}andV2​n+1V_{2n+1}, the one-loop contribution toV2​n+1V_{2n+1}also has a minus sign relative toV2​n+1V_{2n+1}itself. Finally, it is clear that this argument works equally well forV2​n+2V_{2n+2}, whose one-loop correction comes from the vertexV2​n+4V_{2n+4}by contracting two of the internalψ\psifields to form a loop. Thus, lettingVVwith no subscript stand for an arbitrary vertex, we findV(0)+V(1)=+

}}=(1-\mathscr{I})V^{(0)}.(156)

SinceV(0)V^{(0)}is proportional toλ\lambda, we find that result of the one-loop computation can be summarized by simply shiftingλ\lambdain precisely the same manner in all of the vertex factors as well as in the propagator:λ→(1−ℐ)​λ.\lambda\rightarrow(1-\mathscr{I})\lambda.(157)

We contrast this with the argument in[13], which argues that the strict form of the vertices in terms ofλ\lambdaneed not hold as one iterates the dynamical renormalization group treatment. Our computation shows that in fact it does, and is the result of a simple multiplicative renormalization of the couplingλ\lambda, which follows as a direct consequence of the non-linearly realized symmetries and field redundancies and associated Ward identities elaborated upon in SectionIV. Were this not the case, the advection interaction potential would have been deformed into an arbitrary power series at any given loop order, subsequently destroying their trigonometric forms and, along with them, the interpretation ofψ\psias a flock angle and𝐧​(ψ){\mathbf{n}}(\psi)as a flock director.

Before we move on to the discussion of higher orders in the loop expansion, we reiterate that, as will be elaborated upon in AppendixDand as given in (182),λ\lambdais not the only parameter that will end up getting renormalized.

## Appendix CTwo- and Higher-Loop Order

We now move on to consider the effect of higher order loop corrections. From (123) and (124), one straightforwardly derives the relationI=L−1+V,I=L-1+V,(158)

whereV=∑n=1∞(V2​n+1+V2​n+2)V=\sum_{n=1}^{\infty}(V_{2n+1}+V_{2n+2})is the total number of vertices, which is nothing more than a transcription of the fact that the Euler characteristic of the plane is 2, and that the Feynman diagrams in question are planar. The usual expression for the Euler characteristic is given by181818The usage ofχ\chias the symbol for the Euler characteristic is standard. However, it should not be confused with the roughness exponent, which measures the scaling dimension ofψ\psi, i.e.,[ψ]=χ[\psi]=\chi, nor with the originalχ\chifield that we introduced in the MSRDJ procedure before converting toϕ=−i​χ\phi=-i\chi.χ=V−E+F\chi=V-E+F, whereVVis the number of vertices,EEthe number of edges, andFFthe number of faces. We can re-express this in terms ofII, the number of edges, and whereL+1L+1is the number of faces, given that we must count the rest of the plane outside the Feynman diagram as a face. Sinceχ=2−2​g\chi=2-2g, whereggis the genus, and whereχ=2\chi=2for the plane, so that one finally arrives at (158).

ForL=1L=1, we haveI=VI=Vand so it is possible to haveIϕ​ψ=II_{\phi\psi}=Iwith each vertex having exactly oneϕ\phifield, except that the diagram would vanish by the nonrenormalization theorem discussed above. ForL=2L=2, however, this is not possible becauseI=V+1I=V+1. Thus, the maximum value ofIϕ​ψI_{\phi\psi}isI−1I-1. However, with two loops,Iϕ​ψ=I−1I_{\phi\psi}=I-1will force one of the loops to be cyclic inϕ\phiandψ\psiand thus vanish by the nonrenormalization theorem. Therefore, the maximum value forIϕ​ψI_{\phi\psi}is in factI−2I-2. Consequently, the maximum superficial degree of divergence isDmax=8−4​I+3​(I−2)=2−I,D_{\rm max}=8-4I+3(I-2)=2-I,(159)

and so only(I,Iϕ​ψ)=(2,0)(I,I_{\phi\psi})=(2,0)contributes. In other words, there is only one contributing diagram:Ma​b(2)=

whereaaandbbcan beϕ\phiorψ\psi. This vanishes fora=b=ϕa=b=\phianda=b=ψa=b=\psifor the same reason that the one-loop diagrams vanish in those cases. Therefore,Mψ​ψ(0)+Mψ​ψ(1)+Mψ​ψ(2)\displaystyle M_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\psi\psi}^{(1)}+M_{\psi\psi}^{(2)}=0,\displaystyle=0,Mϕ​ϕ(0)+Mϕ​ϕ(1)+Mϕ​ϕ(2)\displaystyle M_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\phi}^{(1)}+M_{\phi\phi}^{(2)}=2​Δ.\displaystyle=2\Delta.(161)

The result for(a,b)=(ϕ,ψ)(a,b)=(\phi,\psi)is thusMϕ​ψ(2)=18​(2​ℐ)2​i​kx=12​ℐ2​i​kx.M_{\phi\psi}^{(2)}=\frac{1}{8}(2\mathscr{I})^{2}ik_{x}=\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}ik_{x}.(162)

Compared to the one-loop diagram, which has a symmetry factor of 2, this diagram has a symmetry factor of8=2L​L!8=2^{L}L!forL=2L=2. The factor ofL!L!comes from permutations of the loops, and where each loop comes with a factor of22since switching the two fields in each internal loop does not actually change the diagram. Therefore:Mϕ​ψ(0)+Mϕ​ψ(1)+Mϕ​ψ(2)=−i​[ω−(1−ℐ+12​ℐ2)​λ​kx+i​κ​k2].M_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+M_{\phi\psi}^{(1)}+M_{\phi\psi}^{(2)}=-i\biggl[\omega-\biggl(1-\mathscr{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}\biggr)\lambda k_{x}+i\kappa k^{2}\biggr].(163)

In other words,λ\lambdarenormalizes toλ→(1−ℐ+12​ℐ2)​λ.\lambda\rightarrow\biggl(1-\mathscr{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}\biggr)\lambda.(164)

By the same argument, the only diagram that contributes to the two-loop vertex correction is the one-loop diagram in (165) but with an extra loop added to the same vertex:V(0)+V(1)+V(2)=++=(1−ℐ+12​ℐ2)​V(0).V^{(0)}+V^{(1)}+V^{(2)}=\vbox{\hbox{

}}=\biggl(1-\mathscr{I}+\frac{1}{2}\mathscr{I}^{2}\biggr)V^{(0)}.(165)

Thus, up to two-loop order, the result of the loop calculation is to shiftλ\lambdain a way which is consistent across its appearance in the propagator and all of the interaction terms. However, again, this does not imply thatλ\lambdais theonlyparameter that gets renormalized, as we’ll flesh out in the next section.

The pattern is clear: In general,V=I−L+IV=I-L+Iand so(Iϕ​ψ)max=V=I−L+1(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}=V=I-L+1just from topological considerations. However, the nonrenormalization theorem implies that(Iϕ​ψ)max(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}is one less than this, given that(Iϕ​ψ)max=V=I−L(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}=V=I-L. Thus, the maximum superficial degree of divergence isDmax=4​(L−I)+3​(Iϕ​ψ)max=L−I.D_{\rm max}=4(L-I)+3(I_{\phi\psi})_{\rm max}=L-I.(166)

SinceI≥LI\geq L, the only possiblity isI=LI=L, which is logarithmically divergent and has the structure ofLLψ\psi-ψ\psiloops attached to one vertex. Therefore, to all-loop order, the shift ofλ\lambdareadsλ→∑L=0∞(−1)L2L​L!​(2​ℐ)L​λ=e−ℐ​λ=(Λμ)−Δ4​π​κ​λ,\lambda\rightarrow\sum_{L=0}^{\infty}\frac{(-1)^{L}}{2^{L}L!}(2\mathscr{I})^{L}\lambda=e^{-\mathscr{I}}\lambda=\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\lambda,(167)

while there are no diagrammatic contributions to theΔ\Deltaorκ\kappaterms.

## C.1Random Phase Approximation

Instead of amputating the corrections to the propagators, we can instead sum all of the one-particle-irreducible (1PI) diagrams and then multiply each by two copies of the bare propagator. The sum of the amputated 1PI diagrams is the analog of the self energyΣ\Sigma. For example,Σϕ​ψ≡∑L=1∞Mϕ​ψ(L)=∑L=1∞(−ℐ)LL!​i​λ​kx=(e−ℐ−1)​i​λ​kx=[(Λμ)−Δ4​π​κ−1]​i​λ​kx.\Sigma_{\phi\psi}\equiv\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}M_{\phi\psi}^{(L)}=\sum_{L=1}^{\infty}\frac{(-\mathscr{I})^{L}}{L!}i\lambda k_{x}=\bigl(e^{-\mathscr{I}}-1\bigr)i\lambda k_{x}=\biggl[\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}-1\biggr]i\lambda k_{x}.(168)

The 1PI correction to the propagator, as opposed to the inverse propagator, is given by the above flanked by one factor of the bare propagator on either side. The Random Phase Approximation (RPA) simply chains any number of these in series and sums them all up. Thus, the renormalized propagator is given by:𝒢ϕ​ψren\displaystyle{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren}=𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)+𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)​Σϕ​ψ​𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)+𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)​Σϕ​ψ​𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)​Σϕ​ψ​𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)+⋯\displaystyle={\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}+\cdots=𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)1−𝒢ϕ​ψ(0)​Σϕ​ψ≡1(𝒢ϕ​ψ(0))−1−Σϕ​ψ,\displaystyle=\frac{{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}}{1-{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\Sigma_{\phi\psi}}\equiv\frac{1}{\bigl({\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\bigr)^{-1}-\Sigma_{\phi\psi}},

which simplifies to𝒢ϕ​ψren=1i​(ω−λ​kx−i​κ​k2)−[(Λμ)−Δ4​π​κ−1]​i​λ​kx=−iω−(Λμ)−Δ4​π​κ​λ​kx−i​κ​k2.{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren}=\frac{1}{i(\omega-\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2})-\bigl[\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\bigr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}-1\bigr]i\lambda k_{x}}=\frac{-i}{\omega-\bigl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\bigr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\lambda k_{x}-i\kappa k^{2}}.(169)

Therefore, the renormalized propagator is the same as the original propagator with theλ\lambdaparameter shifted as in (167). There are no diagrams with purelyψ\psiinternal loops that can contribute toGϕ​ϕ(0)=0G_{\phi\phi}^{(0)}=0, and so that persists in vanishing identically.

The corrections to theψ\psi-ψ\psipropagator take the form of any number of corrections to theψ\psi-ϕ\phipropagator excluding the final bareGψ​ϕ(0)G_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}, which is instead replaced with aGψ​ψ(0)G_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}insertion, followed by any number of corrections to theϕ\phi-ψ\psipropagator, excluding the initial bareGϕ​ψ(0)G_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}, schematically depicted below, where the red solid line is theGψ​ψ(0)G_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}which replaces theGψ​ϕ(0)G_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}, which would have been on the end of the left-hand portion of the diagram, and theGϕ​ψ(0)G_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}, which would have been at the start of the right-hand portion of the diagram:(170)

Again, there can be any number of RPAGψ​ϕG_{\psi\phi}corrections on the left and RPAGϕ​ψG_{\phi\psi}corrections on the right (and need not involve the same number on both sides).
Therefore, this evaluates to𝒢ψ​ψren=𝒢ψ​ϕren​(𝒢ψ​ϕ(0))−1​𝒢ψ​ψ(0)​(𝒢ϕ​ψ(0))−1​𝒢ϕ​ψren.{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}={\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{\rm ren}\bigl({\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}\bigr)^{-1}{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}\bigl({\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)}\bigr)^{-1}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren}.(171)

Using the fact that𝒢ψ​ψ(0)=2​Δ​𝒢ψ​ϕ(0)​𝒢ϕ​ψ(0),{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{(0)}=2\Delta{\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{(0)}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{(0)},(172)

(171) simplifies to𝒢ψ​ψren=2​Δ​𝒢ψ​ϕren​𝒢ϕ​ψren,{\cal G}_{\psi\psi}^{\rm ren}=2\Delta{\cal G}_{\psi\phi}^{\rm ren}{\cal G}_{\phi\psi}^{\rm ren},(173)

which is indeed precisely the same as the bare propagator withλ\lambdashifted as in (167).

Once again, the complete all-loop calculation seems to naïvely indicate that the only quantity that gets renormalized isλ\lambda, which we know cannot be the case as this would violate the Ward identities discussed in Sec.IV. We finally turn towards this recurring point, along with the remaining details of the complete renormalization of the theory in the next section.

## Appendix DWilsonian Renormalization

In what follows, we adopt the Wilsonian perspective on renormalization with hard cutoff regularization191919Given the scheme-independence of the coefficients of the leading logarithmic UV divergences, along with the results of the previous subsections, one can also arrive at the same conclusions by isolating the UV poles via dimensional regularization and integrating the corresponding beta functions.. We split the modes of the angle and response fields,ψ\psiandϕ\phi, into “slow” modes with momentum<Λb<\frac{\Lambda}{b}, whereΛ\Lambdais some UV scale andb>1b>1is a real number, and “fast” modes with momentum betweenΛb\frac{\Lambda}{b}andΛ\Lambda. We integrate out the fast modes, which means that in the result (167), we setμ=Λb\mu=\frac{\Lambda}{b}so that the factor involving the cutoff becomes(Λμ)−Δ4​π​κ→μ→Λ/bb−Δ4​π​κ.\biggl(\frac{\Lambda}{\mu}\biggr)^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}\xrightarrow{\mu\rightarrow\Lambda/b}b^{-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}.(174)

We thenrescalethe momentum back up so as to restore the domain of the slow modes to be between 0 andΛ\Lambda. In so doing, we have to scale all dimensionful quantities according to theirbarescaling dimension. The bare scaling dimensions have been determined in such a way as to force the action to be dimensionless, of course.

Though we performed the loop calculation in momentum space, we can, of course, have just as readily worked in real space, especially since the result was so simple: The renormalized action can at first glance be brought into a form that looks exactly the same as the bare action but withλ\lambdashifted according to (167), or, using (174), according toλ→b−r​λ,r≡Δ4​π​κ.\lambda\rightarrow b^{-r}\lambda,\qquad\qquad r\equiv\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}.(175)

Denoting renormalized quantities with an overline, the results of our loop computation to all orders would seem to naïvely imply thatS¯free\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm free}=−∫t,𝐱ϕ​(t,𝐱)​[∂t+b−r​λ​∂x+κ​∇2]​ψ​(t,𝐱)−Δ​∫t,𝐱ϕ​(t,𝐱)2,\displaystyle=-\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}})\bigl[\partial_{t}+b^{-r}\lambda\partial_{x}+\kappa\nabla^{2}\bigr]\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})-\Delta\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{2},(176a)S¯int(2​n+1)\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm int}^{(2n+1)}=(−1)n(2​n)!​b−r​λ​∫t,𝐱ψ​(t,𝐱)2​n​∂yϕ​(t,𝐱),\displaystyle=\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n)!}b^{-r}\lambda\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{2n}\partial_{y}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}}),(176b)S¯int(2​n+2)\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm int}^{(2n+2)}=−(−1)n(2​n+1)!​b−r​λ​∫t,𝐱ψ​(t,𝐱)2​n+1​∂xϕ​(t,𝐱).\displaystyle=-\frac{(-1)^{n}}{(2n+1)!}b^{-r}\lambda\int_{t,{\mathbf{x}}}\psi(t,{\mathbf{x}})^{2n+1}\partial_{x}\phi(t,{\mathbf{x}}).(176c)

However, this would be incompatible with the Ward identities discussed in SectionIV, which force the anomalous dimension ofλ\lambda,Δ\Delta, andκ\kappato be the same at all loop orders. Those same Ward identities therefore imply thatrrmust also remain dimensionless. Consistency of the renormalization of theϕ\phi-ψ\psicross-terms in the action would then also necessitate that spatial derivatives (or spatial momenta) must themselves also gain anomalous dimensions as well. This latter fact may seem rather striking from a relativistic point of view, and warrants further elaboration. After all, in relativistic quantum field theory, one determines the wave function renormalization by assuming that any correction to the kinetic term must be absorbed entirely by the respective field and not by the spacetime derivatives. If this were not the case, there would be an ambiguity in how to cancel divergences (beyond the usual possibilities of regularization scheme-dependence). Of course, the point is that one is not allowed to alter the scaling of time or energy, because of the operational fact that renormalization is premised on separating out low-energyfrom high-energymodes, and integrating out the latter. In a relativistic theory, one cannot therefore change the scaling of space or momentum either.

On the other hand, in a theory with non-trivial Lifshitz scaling, an anomalous dimension for spatial momentum is implied by the renormalization of the dynamical critical exponent,zz. Were it not possible for spatial momentum to gain an anomalous dimension, it would likewise be impossible forzzto get renormalized. Therefore, given the preceding discussion, we are obliged to consider assigning anomalous dimensions to each ofλ\lambda,Δ\Delta,κ\kappa,ϕ\phiand𝐱{\mathbf{x}}, which we denote asγλ\gamma_{\lambda},γΔ\gamma_{\Delta},γκ\gamma_{\kappa},γϕ\gamma_{\phi}, andγ𝐱\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}, respectively. That is, we define the renormalized parameters:λ¯\displaystyle\overline{\lambda}=bγλ​λ,\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\lambda}}\lambda,Δ¯\displaystyle\overline{\Delta}=bγΔ​Δ,\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\Delta}}\Delta,κ¯\displaystyle\overline{\kappa}=bγκ​κ,\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\kappa}}\kappa,ϕ¯\displaystyle\overline{\phi}=bγϕ​ϕ,\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{\phi}}\phi,𝐱¯\displaystyle\overline{{\mathbf{x}}}=bγ𝐱​𝐱.\displaystyle=b^{\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}}{\mathbf{x}}.(177)

As discussed above, the dimensions of time must remain unchanged, andψ\psimust remain exactly dimensionless in order for the trigonometric interactions to maintain their form, which is a consequence of the shift-rotation subgroup of the symmetry discussed in SectionIV. That is,t¯\displaystyle\overline{t}=t,ψ¯=ψ.\displaystyle=t,\qquad\overline{\psi}=\psi.(178)

Plugging in the above to express the bare quantities in (176a) in terms of renormalized quantities results inS¯free\displaystyle\overline{S}_{\rm free}=−b−2​γ𝐱−γϕ​∫t¯,𝐱¯ϕ¯​(t¯,𝐱¯)​[∂t¯+bγ𝐱−γλ−r​λ¯​∂x¯+b2​γ𝐱−γκ​κ¯​∇¯2]​ψ¯​(t¯,𝐱¯)\displaystyle=-b^{-2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-\gamma_{\phi}}\int_{\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}}}\overline{\phi}(\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}})\bigl[\partial_{\overline{t}}+b^{\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-\gamma_{\lambda}-r}\overline{\lambda}\partial_{\overline{x}}+b^{2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-\gamma_{\kappa}}\overline{\kappa}\overline{\nabla}^{2}\bigr]\overline{\psi}(\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}})−b−2​γ𝐱−2​γϕ−γΔ​Δ¯​∫t¯,𝐱¯ϕ¯​(t¯,𝐱¯)2,\displaystyle\quad-b^{-2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-2\gamma_{\phi}-\gamma_{\Delta}}\overline{\Delta}\int_{\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}}}\overline{\phi}(\overline{t},\overline{{\mathbf{x}}})^{2},(179)

which immediately implies the following relations:γϕ\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}=−2​γ𝐱,\displaystyle=-2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}},γλ\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}=γ𝐱−r,\displaystyle=\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}-r,γκ\displaystyle\gamma_{\kappa}=2​γ𝐱,\displaystyle=2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}},γΔ\displaystyle\gamma_{\Delta}=2​γ𝐱.\displaystyle=2\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}.(180)

Note that consistency requires thatΔ\Deltaandκ\kappahave the same anomalous dimension regardless of the anomalous dimension of space. Were this not the case, the ratiorrwould have failed to remain dimensionless. Again, this is protected by the Ward identities discussed in SectionIV, which also provide the remaining relation,γλ=γΔ.\gamma_{\lambda}=\gamma_{\Delta}.(181)

Solving these equations yieldsγλ\displaystyle\gamma_{\lambda}=γΔ=γκ=−Δ2​π​κ,\displaystyle=\gamma_{\Delta}=\gamma_{\kappa}=-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa},γϕ\displaystyle\gamma_{\phi}=Δ2​π​κ,\displaystyle=\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa},γ𝐱\displaystyle\gamma_{{\mathbf{x}}}=−Δ4​π​κ.\displaystyle=-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}.(182)

The renormalized value ofzzis obtained from the ratio of the fully dressed scaling dimensions of the temporal and spatial coordinates, and is therefore given by:z¯=[t¯][x¯]=−2−1−Δ4​π​κ=21+Δ4​π​κ.\overline{z}=\frac{[\bar{t}]}{[\bar{x}]}=\frac{-2}{-1-\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}=\frac{2}{1+\frac{\Delta}{4\pi\kappa}}.(183)

SinceΔ\Deltaandκ\kappashould be positive, the dynamical critical exponent is indeed less than 2[13].

We note that in[9,13], the authors make the a priori reasonable distinction between the factorλ\lambdathat appears in theϕ\phi-ψ\psiandψ\psi-ϕ\phipropagators and the factor ofλ\lambdathat appears in the interaction vertices. In[13], for example, the former is calledλ1\lambda_{1}and the latter is called−λ2-\lambda_{2}. The reason for this distinction is that althoughλ1=−λ2=λ\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}=\lambdaat the bare level, loop corrections will generically spoil this relation202020The authors go on to argue that there can be no nontrivial fixed point withλ1≠0\lambda_{1}\neq 0andλ2=0\lambda_{2}=0or withλ1=0\lambda_{1}=0andλ2≠0\lambda_{2}\neq 0. That is, any fixed point must haveλ1≠0\lambda_{1}\neq 0andλ2≠0\lambda_{2}\neq 0, which is consistent with our findings.. The only manner in which this relation can be preserved is if it is somehow protected by symmetry. As demonstrated in SectionIV, this protection is in fact provided by a non-linear realization of the one-parameter shift/rotation subgroup in (LABEL:eq:symm) that forces relations between the various vertex factors at all loops, such that the recursion relations (21) hold to all orders, and the trigonometric form of the advection interaction is invariant under RG up to a rescaling ofλ\lambda. That is, all appearances ofλ\lambda, whether in the propagators or in the vertex factors are forced to renormalize in the exact same manner because of this symmetry.

Therefore, indeed, it is true that there cannot be nontrivial fixed point unless bothλ1\lambda_{1}andλ2\lambda_{2}are nonzero, and in fact,λ1=−λ2≡λ\lambda_{1}=-\lambda_{2}\equiv\lambda. However, it is not possible to further specify the precise value ofλ\lambdaat any fixed point or collection of fixed points unless one specifies some quantity that depends onλ\lambdaat some particular scale,μ¯\bar{\mu}to fix the value ofλ\lambdaat that scale as a renormalization condition that subsequently allows us to infer the value ofλ\lambdaat any other scale,μ\mu. Doing so, results in the scale dependent couplingλ​(μ)=(μ¯μ)1−Δ2​π​κ​λ​(μ¯).\lambda(\mu)=\biggl(\frac{\bar{\mu}}{\mu}\biggr)^{1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}}\lambda(\bar{\mu}).(184)

Which can be viewed as the result of having integrated the corresponding beta function:μ​d​λd​μ=−(1−Δ2​π​κ)​λ.\mu\frac{d\lambda}{d\mu}=-\biggl(1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}\biggr)\lambda.(185)

It is more typical to express RG running in terms of an RG scaless, which flows from the UV to the IR (thus encapsulating that it is a monotonic flow resulting from successive coarse graining over short distance modes), and soincreasesasμ\mudecreases. Hence,d​λd​s=(1−Δ2​π​κ)​λ,\frac{d\lambda}{ds}=\biggl(1-\frac{\Delta}{2\pi\kappa}\biggr)\lambda,(186)

wheres=log⁡(μ¯/μ)s=\log(\bar{\mu}/\mu). We stress in closing this appendix that common to all field theoretic computations, there is a degree of arbitrariness with respect to field redefinitions (or changes of variable such that the transformed variable also represents a sufficient statistic) that will propagate onto how individual couplings that parameterize individual operators in the action renormalize. All of this arbitrariness will drop out of physical observables, which is the content of the equivalence theorem for on-shell S-matrix elements in quantum field theory[7,40], but is much more simply the invariance of the measure and generating functional for connected correlation functions in statistical field theories up to a suitable redefinition of the source[21,44]. The final result of our all-loop resummation of the isotropic Malthusian flock is to have elucidated its phase structure, and this will have resulted no matter the particular scheme or operator basis one may have chosen to expand in.

## Appendix ESymmetries and Invariances of Mathusian Flocks

We observe that the MSRDJ action for the Malthusian flock has a very special structure, by first noting that the functionalℰ\mathcal{E}in Eq. (4) can be written asℰ\displaystyle\mathcal{E}=∂μjψμ,\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi},jψt\displaystyle j_{\psi}^{t}=ψ,\displaystyle=\psi,𝐣ψ\displaystyle\mathbf{j}_{\psi}=−κ​∇ψ−λ​𝐧^⟂.\displaystyle=-\kappa\nabla\psi-\lambda\hat{\mathbf{n}}_{\perp}.(187)

Thus, the MSRDJ Lagrangian takes the formℒ=ϕ​∂μjψμ−Δ​ϕ2.{\cal L}=\phi\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}-\Delta\phi^{2}.(188)

Integrating the above by parts indicates that the currentjψμj^{\mu}_{\psi}couples only to∂μϕ\partial_{\mu}\phi, and so it is natural to consider the set of all possible redundanciens of the MSRDJ action under constant shifts of the response field.

Therefore, we consider a transformation of the coordinates with the corresponding transformation ofϕ\phiandjψμj^{\mu}_{\psi}in addition to inhomogeneous contributions:xμ\displaystyle x^{\mu}→x′⁣μ=xμ+ϵμ​(x),\displaystyle\rightarrow x^{\prime\mu}=x^{\mu}+\epsilon^{\mu}(x),(189a)ϕ​(x)\displaystyle\phi(x)→ϕ′​(x′)=ϕ​(x)+c,\displaystyle\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})=\phi(x)+c,(189b)jψμ​(x)\displaystyle j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)→jψ′′⁣μ​(x′)=∂x′⁣μ∂xν​jψν​(x)+ωμ​(x)=jψμ​(x)+∂ϵμ∂xν​jψν​(x)+ωμ​(x),\displaystyle\rightarrow j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})=\frac{\partial x^{\prime\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}j^{\nu}_{\psi}(x)+\omega^{\mu}(x)=j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\frac{\partial\epsilon^{\mu}}{\partial x^{\nu}}j^{\nu}_{\psi}(x)+\omega^{\mu}(x),(189c)

whereωμ\omega^{\mu}is an as yet to be determined contribution induced by transformations ofψ\psithat we aim to solve for by demanding that the action be invariant. We furthermore assume thatϵμ\epsilon^{\mu},cc, andωμ\omega^{\mu}are infinitesimal and we keep only up to linear order in these quantities. Then,∂μjψμ\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}transforms as∂μjψμ​(x)\displaystyle\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)→∂μ′jψ′′⁣μ​(x′)\displaystyle\rightarrow\partial^{\prime}_{\mu}j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})=∂xρ∂x′⁣ν​∂∂xρ​(∂x′⁣ν∂xμ​jψμ​(x)+ων​(x))\displaystyle=\frac{\partial x^{\rho}}{\partial x^{\prime\nu}}\frac{\partial}{\partial x^{\rho}}\biggl(\frac{\partial x^{\prime\nu}}{\partial x^{\mu}}j_{\psi}^{\mu}(x)+\omega^{\nu}(x)\biggr)=∂μjψμ​(x)+∂2ϵν∂xν​∂xμ​jψμ​(x)+∂μωμ​(x).\displaystyle=\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j_{\psi}^{\mu}(x)+\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}(x).(190)

Therefore, the Lagrangian transforms asℒ→ℒ′\displaystyle{\cal L}\rightarrow{\cal L}^{\prime}=ϕ′​(x′)​∂μ′jψ′′⁣μ​(x′)−Δ​ϕ′​(x′)2\displaystyle=\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})\,\partial_{\mu}^{\prime}j_{\psi^{\prime}}^{\prime\mu}(x^{\prime})-\Delta\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})^{2}=[ϕ​(x)+c]​(∂μjψμ​(x)+∂2ϵν∂xν​∂xμ​jψμ​(x)+∂μωμ​(x))−Δ​[ϕ​(x)+c]2\displaystyle=\bigl[\phi(x)+c\bigr]\biggl(\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)+\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}(x)\biggr)-\Delta\bigl[\phi(x)+c\bigr]^{2}=ℒ+c​∂μjψμ+ϕ​(∂μωμ+∂2ϵν∂xν​∂xμ​jψμ​(x)−2​Δ​c).\displaystyle=\mathcal{L}+c\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}+\phi\biggl(\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}+\frac{\partial^{2}\epsilon^{\nu}}{\partial x^{\nu}\partial x^{\mu}}j^{\mu}_{\psi}(x)-2\Delta c\biggr).(191)

Sinceccis a constant, the termc​∂μjψμc\,\partial_{\mu}j^{\mu}_{\psi}is a total derivative. Thus, the action is invariant if∂μωμ=2​Δ​c−(∂μ∂νϵν)​jψμ.\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=2\Delta c-(\partial_{\mu}\partial_{\nu}\epsilon^{\nu})j^{\mu}_{\psi}.(192)

Consider the coordinate transformation given by an anti-clockwise rotation of the Cartesian coordinates by a time-dependent and spatially-independent angleθ​(t)\theta(t):t′\displaystyle t^{\prime}=t,\displaystyle=t,x′\displaystyle x^{\prime}=x−y​θ,\displaystyle=x-y\theta,y′\displaystyle y^{\prime}=y+x​θ.\displaystyle=y+x\theta.(193)

so that the time component ofωμ\omega^{\mu}by definition is the transformation of the flock angle, so that the field transformations are given byδ​ψ≡ψ′​(x′)−ψ​(x)=ωt​(x),δ​ϕ≡ϕ′​(x′)−ϕ​(x)=c.\delta\psi\equiv\psi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})-\psi(x)=\omega^{t}(x),\qquad\delta\phi\equiv\phi^{\prime}(x^{\prime})-\phi(x)=c.(196)

Therefore, using the spatial components ofjμj^{\mu}in Eq. (187), substituting the above, and expanding to first order:jψ′′⁣x\displaystyle j^{\prime x}_{\psi^{\prime}}=−κ​∂x′ψ′+λ​sin⁡ψ′\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{x^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}+\lambda\sin\psi^{\prime}=−κ​(∂x−θ​∂y)​(ψ+ωt)+λ​sin⁡(ψ+ωt)\displaystyle=-\kappa(\partial_{x}-\theta\partial_{y})(\psi+\omega^{t})+\lambda\sin(\psi+\omega^{t})=−κ​∂xψ+κ​θ​∂yψ−κ​∂xωt+λ​sin⁡ψ+λ​ωt​cos⁡ψ\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{x}\psi+\kappa\theta\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\partial_{x}\omega^{t}+\lambda\sin\psi+\lambda\omega^{t}\cos\psi=jψx+κ​θ​∂yψ−κ​∂xωt+λ​ωt​cos⁡ψ,\displaystyle=j^{x}_{\psi}+\kappa\theta\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\partial_{x}\omega^{t}+\lambda\omega^{t}\cos\psi,(197)

andjψ′′⁣y\displaystyle j^{\prime y}_{\psi^{\prime}}=−κ​∂y′ψ′−λ​cos⁡ψ′\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{y^{\prime}}\psi^{\prime}-\lambda\cos\psi^{\prime}=−κ​(∂y+θ​∂x)​(ψ+ωt)−λ​cos⁡(ψ+ωt)\displaystyle=-\kappa(\partial_{y}+\theta\partial_{x})(\psi+\omega^{t})-\lambda\cos(\psi+\omega^{t})=−κ​∂yψ−κ​θ​∂xψ−κ​∂yωt−λ​cos⁡ψ+λ​ωt​sin⁡ψ\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{y}\psi-\kappa\theta\partial_{x}\psi-\kappa\partial_{y}\omega^{t}-\lambda\cos\psi+\lambda\omega^{t}\sin\psi=jψy−κ​θ​∂xψ−κ​∂yωt+λ​ωt​sin⁡ψ.\displaystyle=j^{y}_{\psi}-\kappa\theta\partial_{x}\psi-\kappa\partial_{y}\omega^{t}+\lambda\omega^{t}\sin\psi.(198)

Comparing with Eq. (194b) and (194c), one can solve forωx\omega^{x}andωy\omega^{y}in terms ofωt\omega^{t}, which we redefine for convenience asωt≡δ​ψ:=W\omega^{t}\equiv\delta\psi:=W, so thatωt\displaystyle\omega^{t}=W,\displaystyle=W,(199a)ωx\displaystyle\omega^{x}=−κ​∂xW+λ​(W−θ)​cos⁡ψ+y​θ˙​ψ,\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{x}W+\lambda(W-\theta)\cos\psi+y\dot{\theta}\psi,(199b)ωy\displaystyle\omega^{y}=−κ​∂yW+λ​(W−θ)​sin⁡ψ−x​θ˙​ψ.\displaystyle=-\kappa\partial_{y}W+\lambda(W-\theta)\sin\psi-x\dot{\theta}\psi.(199c)

Taking the divergence of the above, and imposing the condition that guarantees the invariance of the action (192), implies thatW≡δ​ψW\equiv\delta\psimust satisfy:∂μωμ=W˙−κ​∇2W+λ​∇⋅[(W−θ)​𝐧^​(ψ)]+θ˙​(y​∂x−x​∂y)​ψ=2​Δ​c,\partial_{\mu}\omega^{\mu}=\dot{W}-\kappa\nabla^{2}W+\lambda\nabla\cdot\bigl[(W-\theta)\hat{\mathbf{n}}(\psi)\bigr]+\dot{\theta}(y\partial_{x}-x\partial_{y})\psi=2\Delta c,(200)

which can be re-expressed asW˙−κ​∇2W+λ​n^​(ψ)⋅∇W+λ​W​∇⋅n^​(ψ)=2​Δ​c+i​θ˙​L​ψ+λ​θ​∇⋅n^​(ψ).\dot{W}-\kappa\nabla^{2}W+\lambda\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi)\cdot\nabla W+\lambda W\nabla\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi)=2\Delta c+i\dot{\theta}L\psi+\lambda\theta\nabla\cdot\hat{\textbf{n}}(\psi).(201)

As a first order PDE (convection-diffusion), one can always obtain a unique solution given a fixed set of boundary conditions[3,32]. That is, demanding thatϕ→ϕ+c\phi\to\phi+calong with making an infinitesimal time-dependent rotation implies that there will always be a unique solution to (201)WWso thatψ→ψ+W​(t,x,y)\psi\to\psi+W(t,x,y)leaves the action invariant.

One can readily check that the symmetry corresponding to a constant shift inψ\psialong with a constant rotation (i.e.,c=0c=0,θ​(t)=ν\theta(t)=\nu,W=νW=\nu), solves (201). One can also consider the case whereθ=0\theta=0, in which case a transformationϕ→ϕ+c\phi\rightarrow\phi+candψ→ψ+W\psi\rightarrow\psi+Wis guaranteed to exist, and therefore also leaves the action invariant and thus corresponds to a physically indistinguishable configuration. Clearly one can proceed and consider more elaborate spacetime transformations in addition to constant shifts inϕ\phiandsolvefor the shiftWWthat would leave the action invariant, which defines the transformation on the flock angle through the identificationW≡δ​ψW\equiv\delta\psi.

## Appendix FComparisons with Previous Treatments of Flock Scaling

The recent literature presents the interested reader with differing viewpoints regarding the extent to which symmetry arguments constrain the scaling behavior of two-dimensional polar flocks[9,25,13,10,12,11]. Since our treatment deploys an explicit diagrammatic renormalization procedure, it is useful to summarize the assumptions underlying the approaches of[9,10,11]relative to[13,12], and to clarify where and how our conclusions might differ. In addition, while the debate between the two aforementioned sets of authors has dominated the discussion in this area, a notable alternative viewpoint has been proposed by Amoretti et al.[2], which utilizes boost-agnostic hydrodynamics and thermodynamic constraints to derive a different set of relations among the critical exponents.

## F.1Chaté–Solon: Symmetry-Based Non-Renormalization Arguments

In[9], Chaté and Solon analyze the Goldstone-mode dynamics of the ordered phase and argue that certain nonlinear couplings do not get renormalized. In particular, immediately following their Eq. (10), they state that the terms originating from the convective derivative are not expected to receive graphical corrections. In their formulation, these terms are proportional to the parameterλ\lambda, and the argument is based on the generalized Galilean invariance introduced in their Appendix B. Our findings lead us to a different conclusion. Our explicit diagrammatic analysis finds nonvanishing renormalization of the couplings associated with the convective derivative. Consequently, the non-renormalization of these couplings does not appear to follow from the symmetries realized in our formulation, which moreover, protects their functional form under loop corrections. It is worth noting that the generalized Galilean invariance invoked in Ref.[9]is derived within a theory truncated at a particular order in the expansions ofsin⁡ψ\sin\psiandcos⁡ψ\cos\psiand is evidently not a symmetry of the full non-linear theory, as elaborated upon in SectionIV. Whether an analogous symmetry survives in a more general treatment is a separate question. However, regardless of its interpretation, our diagrammatic calculation indicates that the corresponding couplings acquire nonzero anomalous dimensions.

A second point concerns the variance of the noiseΔ\Delta. Chaté and Solon argue that because all interactions are derivatively coupled,Δ\Deltareceives no graphical corrections and therefore acquires no anomalous dimension. We agree with the first statement: in our calculation,Δ\Deltaindeed receives no direct diagrammatic corrections. However, we arrive at a different conclusion regarding the second statement. Even in the absence of direct graphical corrections, the scaling dimension ofΔ\Deltacan be modified through its coupling to other running parameters, including the nonlinear couplings, and this is due to certain subtleties of the renormalization procedure in boost non-invariant theories with non-trivial Lifshitz scaling (i.e. withz≠1z\neq 1), as discussed in AppendixD. Thus, the absence of explicit diagrams contributing toΔ\Deltadoes not, by itself, imply that it does not acquire an anomalous dimension.

These assumptions lead directly to the exponent relations proposed in[9]. For Malthusian flocks, Chaté and Solon derive three relations among the scaling exponents(χ,ζ,z)(\chi,\zeta,z), from which they obtain exact exponent values. Their first relation,2​χ+z−ζ=0,2\chi+z-\zeta=0,(202)

follows from the claim that the couplingλx\lambda_{x}multiplying the interactionλx​∂x(ψ3)\lambda_{x}\partial_{x}(\psi^{3})receives no anomalous dimension. Their second relation,χ+z−1=0,\chi+z-1=0,(203)

follows from the analogous claim for the couplingλy\lambda_{y}multiplyingλy​∂yψ2\lambda_{y}\partial_{y}\psi^{2}. In both cases, the justification is the generalized Galilean invariance discussed in Appendix B of Ref.[9]. Their third relation,z−2​χ−1−ζ=0,z-2\chi-1-\zeta=0,(204)

follows from the assumption thatΔ\Deltaacquires no anomalous dimension because no diagrams contribute directly to its renormalization. Thus, they derive the critical exponentsz\displaystyle z=54,\displaystyle=\frac{5}{4},ζ\displaystyle\zeta=34,\displaystyle=\frac{3}{4},χ\displaystyle\chi=−14,\displaystyle=-\frac{1}{4},(205)

with which we clearly differ.

The generalized Galilean invariance employed in Ref.[9]is expressed through their Eq. (B1),ψ′​(𝐫,t)=ψ​(𝐫−𝐫c,t)+ψ0,\psi^{\prime}(\mathbf{r},t)=\psi(\mathbf{r}-\mathbf{r}_{c},t)+\psi_{0},(206)

with∂txc=2​λx​ψ0,∂tyc=λy​ψ0.\partial_{t}x_{c}=2\lambda_{x}\psi_{0},\qquad\partial_{t}y_{c}=\lambda_{y}\psi_{0}.(207)

Since the symmetry structure adopted in the present work differs from that used in Ref.[9], and moreover, is valid for the full non-linear theory, it is perhaps unsurprising that our renormalization analysis does not support the resulting non-renormalization conditions forλx\lambda_{x}andλy\lambda_{y}.

## F.2Chenet al.: Limitations of Symmetry Constraints and Dynamical RG

In[13], the authors revisit the derivation of exact exponent relations. Their principal conclusion is that, for Malthusian flocks, only two independent exponent relations can be established. Consequently, they argue that the three critical exponents cannot be determined exactly. An important point is that the disagreement between Chenet al.and Chaté–Solon is narrower than it may initially appear. Both groups agree on two exponent relations. Indeed, one of the relations obtained by Chenet al.is equivalent to the difference between the first two equations in Eq. (11) of[9],χ−ζ+1=0.\chi-\zeta+1=0.(208)

The primary disagreement concerns the existence of a third independent relation. Chenet al.also accept thatΔ\Deltareceives no direct graphical corrections and therefore recover the relationz−2​χ−1−ζ=0z-2\chi-1-\zeta=0(209)

using essentially the same reasoning as Chaté and Solon. Their departure from[9]instead concerns the nonlinear couplings. Rather than arguing thatλx\lambda_{x}andλy\lambda_{y}have vanishing anomalous dimensions, Chenet al.argue only that their anomalous dimensions must be equal because both couplings originate from the same underlying parameterλ\lambda. Equating the anomalous dimensions ofλx\lambda_{x}andλy\lambda_{y}yields a single exponent relation, whereas setting both anomalous dimensions individually to zero (as done in[9]) yields two. This difference is ultimately responsible for the differing conclusions regarding the exact determination of the Malthusian-flock exponents.

For immortal flocks, the disagreement becomes more substantial. Chenet al.argue that the currently known symmetries of flocking theories are insufficient to derive any exact relations among the scaling exponents. Consequently, they conclude that no exact exponent values can presently be inferred from symmetry considerations alone. Chaté and Solon, while not explicitly deriving exact exponent relations for immortal flocks, argue that the renormalization-group treatment of Chenet al.does not establish such a no-go theorem and therefore does not exclude the possibility that additional symmetry-based constraints may exist.

## F.3Amoretti et al.: Leveraging Thermodynamic Relations

In[2], the authors make use of thermodynamic relations among the various constants appearing in the hydrodynamic equations (the generalized continuity equation and Euler equation, corresponding to their equations 13a and 13b, respectively) to derive two critical-exponent relations, which are distinct from those of the previous two sets of authors. This argument proceeds by first relating all of these coefficients to parameters and transport coefficients of boost-agnostic hydrodynamics and then claiming that these relations should be preserved under RG. The relevant constants split into two sets of like scaling dimension and setting these dimensions to 0 gives the two relationsz−1+χ\displaystyle z-1+\chi=0,\displaystyle=0,z−ζ+χ\displaystyle z-\zeta+\chi=0.\displaystyle=0.(210)

By the same argument as in the other works, namely thatΔ\Deltagains no anomous dimenion, these authors again get the relationz−2​χ+1−d−ζ=0,z-2\chi+1-d-\zeta=0,(211)

whereddis the number of spatial dimensions. This relation reduces to Eq. (204) and (209) of the previous two works whend=2d=2. Thus, their critical exponents arez\displaystyle z=43,\displaystyle=\frac{4}{3},ζ\displaystyle\zeta=1,\displaystyle=1,χ=−13.\displaystyle\chi=-\frac{1}{3}.(212)

We agree withζ=1\zeta=1, but not with the other two critical exponents.

It should be noted that this work utilizes the generalized continuity equation. That is the evolution equation for density perturbations, which should be ignored for Malthusian flocks. In fact, five out of the eight coefficients considered in this work involve density fluctuations and should be removed in the Malthusian theory. Of the eight coefficients that they consider, only two coefficients of dimensionz−1+χz-1+\chi(theirg4g_{4}andg5g_{5}) and one coefficient of dimensionz−ζ+χz-\zeta+\chi(theirw3w_{3}) do not multiply terms involving the density fluctuation. However,w3w_{3}is a coefficient appearing in the evolution equation of the density fluctuation. Therefore, if that is to be dropped altogether for Malthusian flocks, then only their relationz−1+χ=0z-1+\chi=0would remain. Note that this would be consistent with Eq. (203) of Chaté and Solon, though it would lack a third equation to completely determine the values of the critical exponents.

## F.4Relation to this Work

The present analysis differs from these three approaches in that it is based on a different set of symmetries and field redundancies as those considered in[9]and a different set of renormalization conditions, particularly as pertains to theΔ\Deltaparameter, as in all three approaches. We back up the RG predictions derived from the associated Ward identities with an explicit diagrammatic renormalization procedure. Of course, we cannot claim to arrive at any firm conclusions beyond the confines of the very symmetric limit in which we have worked, and much more work remains to be done before we can address any of the relevant questions away from this limit.
